Probabilistic Call By Push Value
Thomas Ehrhard, Christine Tasson

Abstract
. We introduce a probabilistic extension of Levy’s Call-By-Push-Value. This extension consists simply in adding a “flipping coin” boolean closed atomic expression. This language can be understood as a major generalization of Scott’s PCF encompassing both call-by-name and call-by-value and featuring recursive (possibly lazy) data types. We interpret the language in the previously introduced denotational model of probabilistic coherence spaces, a categorical model of full classical Linear Logic, interpreting data types as coalgebras for the resource comonad. We prove adequacy and full abstraction, generalizing earlier results to a much more realistic and powerful programming language.

classifications

1.
Introduction

Call-by-Push-Value

[
22
]
is a class of functional languages
generalizing the lambda-calculus in several directions. From the point of view
of Linear Logic we understand it as a half-polarized system bearing some
similarities with
e.g.
classical Parigot’s lambda-mu-calculus, this is why we
call it
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}{}
. The main idea of Laurent and Regnier interpretation of
call-by-name lambda-mu in Linear Logic
[
20
]
(following
actually
[
13
]
) is that all types of the minimal fragment of the
propositional calculus (with
\Rightarrow
as unique connective) are
interpreted as
negative
types of Linear Logic which are therefore
naturally equipped with structural morphisms: technically speaking, these types
are algebras of the
\wn
-monad of Linear Logic. This additional structure of
negative types allows to perform logical structural rules on the
right
side
of typing judgments even if these formulas are not necessarily of shape

\wn\sigma
, and this is the key towards giving a computational content to
classical logical rules, generalizing the fundamental discovery of Griffin on
typing
\mathsf{call/cc}{}
with Peirce Law
[
15
]
.

From our point of view, the basic idea of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}{}
is quite similar, though
somehow dual and used in a less systematic way: data types are interpreted as

positive
types of Linear Logic equipped therefore with structural
morphisms (as linear duals of negative formulas, they are coalgebras of the

\oc
-comonad) and admit therefore structural rules on the
left side
of
typing judgment even if they are not of shape
\oc\sigma
. This means that a
function defined on a data type can have a
linear function type
even if
it uses its argument in a non-linear way: this non-linearity is automatically
implemented by means of the structural morphisms the positive data type is
equipped with.

The basic positive type in Linear Logic is
\oc\sigma
(where
\sigma
is any
type): it is the very idea of Girard’s call-by-name translation of the
lambda-calculus into Linear Logic to represent the implication type

\sigma\Rightarrow\tau
by means of the decomposition

{\oc\sigma}\multimap{\tau}
. The new idea of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}{}
is to generalize this use
of the linear implication to any type construction of shape

{\varphi}\multimap{\tau}
where
\varphi
is a positive type, without imposing any
linearity restriction on the usage of the argument of type
\varphi
used by a
function of type
{\varphi}\multimap{\tau}
. This non-symmetrical restriction in the
use of the linear implication motivates our description of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
as a
“half-polarized” system: in a fully polarized system like Laurent’s

Polarized Linear Logic
LLP
[
20
]
, one would also
require the type
\sigma
to be negative in
{\varphi}\multimap{\sigma}
(the last system
presented in
[
5
]
implements this idea)
and the resulting formalism would host classical computational primitives such
as
\mathsf{call/cc}
as well. The price to pay, as illustrated
in
[
2
]
, is a less direct access to data types: it is
impossible to give a function from integers to integers the expected type

{\iota}\multimap{\iota}
(where
\iota
is the type of flat natural numbers
satisfying
\iota={\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\iota}
), the simplest type one can give to such a
term is
{\iota}\multimap{\wn\iota}
which complicates its denotational
interpretation
1
1

1

One can also consider
\wn
as the computational monad
of linear continuations and use a translation from direct style into monadic
style (which, for this monad, is just a version of the familiar CPS
translation). This is just a matter of presentation and of syntactic sugar
and does not change the denotational interpretation in the kind of concrete
models of Linear Logic we have in mind such as the relational model, the
coherence space model etc.
.

Not being polarized on the right side of implications,
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
remains
“intuitionistic” just as standard functional programming languages whose
paradigmatic example is
\mathsf{PCF}
. So what is the benefit of this
special status given to positive formulas considered as “data types”? There
are several answers to this question.

•

First, and most importantly, it gives a
call-by-value
access
to data types: when translating
\mathsf{PCF}
into Linear Logic,
the simplest type for a function from integers to integers is

{\oc\iota}\multimap{\iota}
. This means that arguments of type

\iota
are used in a call-by-name way: such arguments are evaluated
again each time they are used. This can of course be quite
inefficient. It is also simply
wrong
if we extend our
language with a random integer generator since in that case each
evaluation of such an argument can lead to a different value: in

\mathsf{PCF}
there is no way to keep memory of the value obtained for one
evaluation of such a parameter and probabilistic programming is
therefore impossible. In
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
data types such as
\iota
can be
accessed in call-by-value, meaning that they are evaluated once and
that the resulting value is kept for further computation: this is
typically the behavior of a function of type

{\iota}\multimap{\iota}
. This is not compulsory however and an explicit

\oc
type constructor still allows to define functions of type

{\oc\iota}\multimap{\iota}
in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
, with the usual
\mathsf{PCF}

behavior.

•

Positive types being closed under positive Linear Logic
connectives (direct sums and tensor product) and under “least
fixpoint” constructions, it is natural to allow corresponding
constructions of positive types in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
as well, leading to a
language with rich data type constructions (various kinds of trees,
streams etc are freely available) and can be accessed in
call-by-value as explained above for integers. From this data types
point of view, the
\oc
Linear Logic connective corresponds to the
type of
suspensions
or
thunks
which are boxes (in the
usual Linear Logic sense) containing unevaluated pieces of program.

•

As already mentioned,
since the Linear Logic type constructors
{}\multimap{}
and
\oc

are available in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
(with the restriction explained above on
the use of
{}\multimap{}
that the left side type must be positive),
one can represent in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
both Girard’s translations from
lambda-calculus into Linear Logic introduced in
[
12
]
: the
usual one which is call-by-name and the “boring one” which is
call-by-value.
So our language
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
is not intrinsically call-by-value and hosts
very simply Girard’s representation of call-by-name in Linear Logic
as further explained in Section
2.4
.

Concretely, in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
, a term of positive type can be a
value
, and then
it is discardable and duplicable and, accordingly, its denotational
interpretation is a morphism of coalgebras: values are particular terms whose
interpretation is easily checked to be such a morphism, which doesn’t preclude
other terms of positive type to have the same property of course, in particular
terms reducing to values! Being a value is a property which can be decided in
time at most the size of the term and values are freely duplicable and
discardable. The “
\beta
-rules” of the calculus (the standard

\beta
-reduction as well as the similar reduction rules associated with tensor
product and direct sum) are subject to restrictions on certain subterms of
redexes to be values (because they must be duplicable and discardable) and
these restrictions make sense thanks to this strong decidability property of
being a value.

Probabilities in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
.

Because of the possibility offered by
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
of handling values in a
call-by-value manner, this language is particularly suitable for
probabilistic functional programming. Contrarily to the common monadic
viewpoint on effects, we consider an extension of the language where
probabilistic choice is a primitive
\mathsf{coin}(p)
of type
{\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}

(the type of booleans)
2
2

2

And not of type
T({\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}})

where
T
would be a computational monad of probabilistic
computations.
parameterized by
p\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
which is
the probability of getting
\mathbf{t}
(and
1-p
is the probability of
getting
\mathbf{f}
). So our probabilistic extension
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}

is in direct style, but, more importantly, the denotational semantics
we consider is itself in “direct style” and does not rely on any
auxiliary computational monad of probability distributions
[
29
,
17
]
(see
[
18
]
for the
difficulties related with the monadic approach to probabilistic
computations), random variables
[
3
,
14
,
25
]
, or measures
[
31
,
16
]
.

On the contrary, we interpret our language in the model of
probabilistic
coherence spaces

[
4
]
that we already used for providing a
fully abstract semantics for probabilistic

\mathsf{PCF}

[
9
]
. A probabilistic coherence space
X
is
given by an at most countable set
|{X}|
(the web of
X
) and a set
\mathsf{P}{X}

of
|{X}|
-indexed families of non-negative real numbers, to be considered as
some kind of “generalized probability distributions”. This set of families of
real numbers is subject to a closure property implementing a simple intuition
of probabilistic observations. Probabilistic coherence spaces are a model of
classical Linear Logic and can be seen as
\omega
-continuous domains equipped
with an operation of convex linear combination, and the linear morphisms of
this model are exactly the Scott continuous functions commuting with these
convex linear combinations.

Besides, probabilistic coherence spaces can be seen as particular

d-cones

[
32
]
and even

Kegelspitzen

[
19
]
, that is, complete partial orders
equipped with a Scott continuous “convex structure” allowing to
compute probabilistic linear combinations of their elements.
Kegelspitzen have been used recently by Rennela to define a
denotational model of a probabilistic extension of
FPC
[
28
]
. The main difference with respect to our approach
seems to be the fact that non-linear morphisms (corresponding to
morphisms of type
{\oc{\sigma}}\multimap{\tau}
in our setting) are
general Scott continuous functions in Rennela’s model
3
3

3

More
precisely, in his interpretation of FPC, Rennela uses strict Scott
continuous functions, but, along the same lines, it seems clear that
Kegelspitzen give rise to a model of PCF where morphisms are general
Scott continuous functions.
, whereas they are analytic
functions
4
4

4

Meaning that it is definable by a power series.
in
ours, which can be seen as very specific Scott continuous
functions. See also
[
11
]
where these functions
are seen to be
stable
in a generalized sense.

As shown in
[
4
]
probabilistic coherence spaces have
all the required completeness properties for interpreting recursive
type definitions (that we already used in
[
7
]

for interpreting the pure lambda-calculus) and so we are able to
associate a probabilistic coherence space with all types of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
.

In this model the type
{\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}
is interpreted as the set of
sub-probability distributions on
\{\mathbf{t},\mathbf{f}\}
so that we have a
straightforward interpretation of
\mathsf{coin}(p)
. Similarly the type of
flat integers
\iota
is interpreted as a probabilistic coherence space
\mathsf{N}

such that
|{\mathsf{N}}|={\mathbb{N}}
and
\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}}
is the set of all probability
distributions on the natural numbers. Given probabilistic spaces
X
and
Y
,
the space
{X}\multimap{Y}
has
|{X}|\times|{Y}|
as web and
\mathsf{P}{({X}\multimap{Y})}

is the set of all
|{X}|\times|{Y}|
matrices which, when applied to an
element of
\mathsf{P}{X}
gives an element of
\mathsf{P}{Y}
. The web of the space
\oc{X}
is the set of all finite multisets of elements of
|{X}|
so that an
element of
{\oc{X}}\multimap{Y}
can be considered as a power series on as many
variables as there are elements in
|{X}|
(the composition law associated
with the Kleisli category of the
\oc
-comonad is compatible with this
interpretation of morphisms as power series).

From a syntactic point of view, the only values of
{\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}
are
\mathbf{t}

and
\mathbf{f}
, so
\mathsf{coin}(p)
is not a value. Therefore we cannot reduce

\langle\lambda x^{{\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}}\,M\rangle\mathsf{coin}(p)
to
{M}\left[{\mathsf{coin}(p)}/{x}\right]
and this
is a good thing since then we would face the problem that the boolean values of
the various occurrences of
\mathsf{coin}(p)
might be different. We have first to
reduce
\mathsf{coin}(p)
to a value, and the reduction rules of our probabilistic

\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
stipulate that
\mathsf{coin}(p)
reduces to
\mathbf{t}
with probability
p
and to

\mathbf{f}
with probability
1-p
(in accordance with the kind of operational
semantics that we considered in our earlier work on this topic, starting
with
[
4
]
). So
\langle\lambda x^{{\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}}\,M\rangle\mathsf{coin}(p)

reduces to
{M}\left[{\mathbf{t}}/{x}\right]
with probability
p
and to
{M}\left[{\mathbf{f}}/{x}\right]

with probability
1-p
, which is perfectly compatible with the intuition that
in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
application is a linear operation (and that implication is linear:
the type of
\lambda x^{{\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}}\,M
is
{({\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}})}\multimap{\sigma}

for some type
\sigma
): in this operational semantics as well as in the
denotational semantics outlined above, linearity corresponds to commutation
with (probabilistic) convex linear combinations.

Contents.

The results presented in this paper illustrate the tight connection between the
syntactic and the denotational intuitions underpinning our understanding of
this calculus.

We first introduce in Section
2
the syntax and operational
semantics of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
, an abstract programming language very close to Paul
Levy’s Call by Push Value (CBPV)
[
22
]
. It however differs from Levy’s
language mainly by the fact that CBPV computation types products and recursive
type definitions have no counterparts in our language. This choice is mainly
motivated by the wish of keeping the presentation reasonably short. It is
argued in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
that
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}

is expressive enough for containing well behaved lazy data types such as the
type of streams, and for encoding call-by-name languages with products.

In Section
3
, we present the Linear Logic model of probabilistic
coherence spaces, introducing mainly the underlying linear category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
,
where
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
general types are interpreted, and the Eilenberg-Moore category

\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
, where the positive types are interpreted. In order to simplify the
Adequacy and Full Abstraction proofs, we restrict actually our attention to a
well-pointed subcategory of
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
whose objects we call “dense
coalgebras”: this will allow to consider all morphisms as functions. As
suggested by one of the referees, there are probably smaller well-pointed
subcategories of
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
where we might interpret our positive types, and in
particular the category of families introduced in
[
1
]

describing call-by-value games. This option will be explored in further
work. We prefer here to work with the most general setting as it is also
compatible with a probabilistic extension of the last system of
[
5
]
, which features classical
\mathsf{call/cc}{}
-like capabilities.

We prove then in Section
4
an Adequacy Theorem whose
statement is extremely simple: given a closed term
M
of type
\mathbf{1}

(which has exactly one value
()
), the denotational semantics of

M
, which is an element of
[0,1]
, coincides with its probability to
reduce to
()
(such a term can only diverge or reduce to

()
). In spite of its simple statement the proof of this result
requires some efforts mainly because of the presence of
unrestricted
5
5

5

Meaning that recursive type definitions are not
restricted to covariant types.
recursive types in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
. The
method used in the proof relies on an idea of Pitts
[
26
]
and
is described in the introduction of Section
4
.

Last we prove Full Abstraction in Section
5
adapting the
technique used in
[
7
]
to the present
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}

setting. The basic idea consists in associating, with any element
a

of the web of the probabilistic coherence space
[{\sigma}]

interpreting the type
\sigma
, a term
a^{-}
of type
6
6

6

For
technical reasons and readability of the proof, the type we give to

a^{-}
in Section
5
slightly differs from this one.

{\oc\sigma}\multimap{{\oc\iota}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}}
such that, given two
elements
w
and
w^{\prime}
of
\mathsf{P}{[{\sigma}]}
such that

w_{a}\not=w^{\prime}_{a}
, the elements
\left[{a^{-}}\right]{}w^{!}
and

\left[{a^{-}}\right]{}(w^{\prime})^{!}
of
\mathsf{P}{({\oc\iota}\multimap{\mathbf{1}})}

are different power series depending on a finite number
n
of
parameters (this number
n
depends actually only on
a
) so that we
can find a rational number valued sub-probability distribution for
these parameters where these power series take different values in

[0,1]
. Applying this to the case where
w
and
w^{\prime}
are the
interpretations of two closed terms
M
and
M^{\prime}
of type
\sigma
, we
obtain, by combining
a^{-}
with the rational sub-probability
distribution which can be represented in the syntax using
\mathsf{coin}(p)

for various values of
p
, a
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
closed term
C
of type

{\oc\sigma}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}
such that the probability of convergence
of
\langle C\rangle M^{\mathord{\oc}}
and
\langle C\rangle(M^{\prime})^{\mathord{\oc}}
are different (by
adequacy). This proves that if two (closed) terms are operationally
equivalent then they have the same semantics in probabilistic
coherence spaces, that is, equational full abstraction.

Further developments.

These results are similar to the ones reported in
[
10
]

but are actually different, and there is no clear logical connection between
them, because the languages are quite different, and therefore, the observation
contexts also. And this even in spite of the fact that
\mathsf{PCF}
can be faithfully
encoded in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
. This seems to show that the semantical framework for
probabilistic functional programming offered by probabilistic coherence spaces
is very robust and deserves further investigations. One major outcome of the
present work is a natural extension of probabilistic computation to rich
data-types, including types of potentially infinite values (streams etc).

Our full abstraction result cannot be extended to inequational full abstraction
with respect to the natural order relation on the elements of probabilistic
coherence spaces: a natural research direction will be to investigate other
(pre)order relations and their possible interactive definitions. Also, it is
quite tempting to replace the equality of probabilities in the definition of
contextual equivalence by a distance; this clearly requires further
developments.

2.
Probabilistic Call By Push Value

We introduce the syntax of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
of CBPV (where HP stands for
“half polarized”).

Types are given by the following BNF syntax. We define by mutual
induction two kinds of types:
positive types
and
general
types
, given type variables
\zeta
,
\xi
…:

\displaystyle\text{positive}\quad\varphi,\psi,\dots{}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathbf{1}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\oc\sigma\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,{\varphi}\otimes{\psi}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,{\varphi}\oplus{\psi}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\zeta\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi

(1)

\displaystyle\text{general}\quad\sigma,\tau\dots{}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\varphi\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,{\varphi}\multimap{\sigma}

(2)

The type
\mathbf{1}
is the
neutral element of
\otimes
and it might seem natural to have also a
type
\mathbf{0}
as the neutral element of
\oplus
. We didn’t do so
because there is no associated constructor in the syntax of terms, and
the only closed terms of type
\mathbf{0}
that one can write are
ever-looping terms.

Observe also that there are no restrictions on the variance of types in
the recursive type construction: for instance, in
\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi
is
a well-formed positive type if
\varphi=\oc({\zeta}\multimap{\zeta})
, where

\zeta
has a negative and a positive occurrence. Do well notice that
our “positive types” are positive in the sense of logical
polarities, and
not
of the variance of type variables!

Terms are given by the following BNF syntax, given variables

x,y,\dots
:

\displaystyle M,N\dots{}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,()\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,M^{\mathord{\oc}}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\left(M,N\right)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{in}_{\ell}M\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{in}_{r}M

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\lambda x^{\varphi}\,M\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\langle M\rangle N\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{case}(M,x_{\ell}\cdot N_{\ell},x_{r}\cdot N_{r})

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}M\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{pr}_{r}M\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{der}\,M\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{fix}\,x^{\oc\sigma}\,M

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{fold}(M)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{unfold}(M)

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{coin}(p),\;p\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}

Remark
.

This functional language
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
, or rather the sublanguage
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}

which is
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
stripped from the
\mathsf{coin}(p)
construct, is formally
very close to Levy’s CBPV. As explained in the Introduction, our
intuition however is more related to Linear Logic than to CBPV and
its general adjunction-based models. This explains why our syntax
slightly departs from Levy’s original syntax as described

e.g.
in
[
21
]
and is much closer to the SFPL language
of
[
23
]
: Levy’s type constructor
F
is kept
implicit and
U
is “
\oc
”. We use LL inspired notations:

M^{\mathord{\oc}}
corresponds to
\mathsf{thunk}(M)
and
\mathsf{der}\,M
to

\mathsf{force}(M)
. Our syntax is also slightly simpler than that
of SFPL in that our general types do not feature products and
recursive types definitions, we will explain in
Section
2.4
that this is not a serious limitation
in terms of expressiveness.

Figure
1
provides the typing rules for these
terms. A typing context is an expression

\mathcal{P}=(x_{1}:\varphi_{1},\dots,x_{k}:\varphi_{k})
where all types are positive
and the
x_{i}
s are pairwise distinct variables.

\mathcal{P},x:\varphi\vdash x:\varphi

\mathcal{P},x:\varphi\vdash M:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash\lambda x^{\varphi}\,M:{\varphi}\multimap{\sigma}

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:{\varphi}\multimap{\sigma}

\mathcal{P}\vdash N:\varphi

\mathcal{P}\vdash\langle M\rangle N:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash M^{\mathord{\oc}}:\oc\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash():\mathbf{1}

\mathcal{P}\vdash M_{\ell}:\varphi_{\ell}

\mathcal{P}\vdash M_{r}:\varphi_{r}

\mathcal{P}\vdash\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right):{\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}}

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\varphi_{i}\quad\small i\in\{\ell,r\}

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{in}_{i}M:{\varphi_{\ell}}\oplus{\varphi_{r}}

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\oc\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{der}\,M:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:{\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}}\quad\small i\in\{\ell,r\}

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{pr}_{i}M:\varphi_{i}

\mathcal{P},x:\oc\sigma\vdash M:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{fix}\,x^{\oc\sigma}\,M:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:{\varphi_{\ell}}\oplus{\varphi_{r}}

\mathcal{P},x_{\ell}:\varphi_{\ell}\vdash M_{\ell}:\sigma

\mathcal{P},x_{r}:\varphi_{r}\vdash M_{r}:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{case}(M,x_{\ell}\cdot M_{\ell},x_{r}\cdot M_{r}):\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{coin}(p):{\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:{\psi}\left[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi}/{\zeta}\right]

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{fold}(M):\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{unfold}(M):{\psi}\left[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi}/{\zeta}\right]

Figure 1.
Typing system for
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}

2.1.
Reduction rules

Values
are particular
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
terms (they are not a new
syntactic category) defined by the following BNF syntax:

\displaystyle V,W\dots{}\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,()\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,M^{\mathord{\oc}}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\left(V,W\right)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{in}_{\ell}V\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{in}_{r}V\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{fold}(V)\,.

Figure
2
defines a deterministic
weak
reduction
relation
\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}
and a probabilistic reduction
\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}
relation.
This reduction is weak in the sense that we never reduce within a “box”
M^{\mathord{\oc}}
or under a
\lambda
.

The distinguishing feature of this reduction system is the role played by
values in the definition of
\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}
. Consider for instance the case of the term

\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right)
; one might expect this term to reduce directly to

M_{\ell}
but this is not the case. One needs first to reduce
M_{\ell}

and

M_{r}
to values before extracting the first component of the pair (the terms

\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right)
and
M_{\ell}
have not the same denotational
interpretation in general). Of course replacing
M_{i}
with
M_{i}^{\mathord{\oc}}
allows
a lazy behavior. Similarly, in the
\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}
rule for
\mathsf{case}
, the term
on which the test is made must be reduced to a value (necessarily of shape
\mathsf{in}_{\ell}V
or
\mathsf{in}_{r}V
if the expression is well typed) before the reduction is performed. As
explained in the Introduction this allows to “memoize” the value
V
for
further usage: the value is passed to the relevant branch of the

\mathsf{case}
through the variable
x_{i}
.

Given two terms
M
,
M^{\prime}
and a real number
p\in[0,1]
,
M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

means that
M
reduces in one step to
M^{\prime}
with probability
p
.

We say that
M
is
weak normal
if there is no reduction

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}
. It is clear that any value is weak normal. When
M
is
closed,
M
is weak normal iff it is a value or an abstraction.

In order to simplify the presentation we
choose
in
Figure
2
a reduction strategy. For instance we decide
that, for reducing
\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right)
to a value, one needs first to reduce

M_{\ell}
to a value, and then
M_{r}
; this choice is of course completely
arbitrary. A similar choice is made for reducing terms of shape
\langle M\rangle N
,
where we require the argument to be reduced first. This choice is less
arbitrary as it will simplify a little bit the proof of adequacy in
Section
4
(see for instance the proof of
Lemma
22
).

We could perfectly define a more general weak reduction relation as
in
[
5
]
for which we could prove a “diamond” confluence
property but we would then need to deal with a reduction transition
system where, at each node (term), several probability distributions
of reduced terms are available and so we would not be able to describe
reduction as a simple (infinite dimensional) stochastic matrix. We
could certainly also define more general reduction rules allowing to
reduce redexes anywhere in terms (apart for
\mathsf{coin}(p)
which can be
reduced only when in linear position) but this would require the
introduction of additional
\sigma
-rules as
in
[
6
]
. As in that paper, confluence can
probably be proven, using ideas coming
from
[
8
,
33
]
for dealing with reduction in
an algebraic lambda-calculus setting.

\mathsf{der}\,M^{\mathord{\oc}}\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}M

\langle\lambda x^{\varphi}\,M\rangle V\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}{M}\left[{V}/{x}\right]

i\in\{\ell,r\}

\mathsf{pr}_{i}\left(V_{\ell},V_{r}\right)\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}V_{i}

\mathsf{fix}\,x^{\oc\sigma}\,M\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}{M}\left[{(\mathsf{fix}\,x^{\oc\sigma}\,M)^{\mathord{\oc}}}/{x}\right]

i\in\{\ell,r\}

\mathsf{case}(\mathsf{in}_{i}V,x_{\ell}\cdot M_{\ell},x_{r}\cdot M_{r})\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}{M_{i}}\left[{V}/{x_{i}}\right]

\mathsf{unfold}(\mathsf{fold}(V))\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}V

M\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}M^{\prime}

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\mathsf{coin}(p)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{in}_{\ell}()

\mathsf{coin}(p)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1-p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{in}_{r}()

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\mathsf{der}\,M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{der}\,M^{\prime}

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\langle M\rangle V\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle M^{\prime}\rangle V

N\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}N^{\prime}

\langle M\rangle N\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle M\rangle N^{\prime}

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

i\in\{\ell,r\}

\mathsf{pr}_{i}M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{pr}_{i}M^{\prime}

M_{\ell}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}_{\ell}

\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\left(M^{\prime}_{\ell},M_{r}\right)

M_{r}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}_{r}

\left(V,M_{r}\right)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\left(V,M^{\prime}_{r}\right)

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

i\in\{\ell,r\}

\mathsf{in}_{i}M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{in}_{i}M^{\prime}

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\mathsf{case}(M,x_{\ell}\cdot M_{\ell},x_{r}\cdot M_{r})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{case}(M^{\prime},x_{\ell}\cdot M_{\ell},x_{r}\cdot M_{r})

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\mathsf{fold}(M)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{fold}(M^{\prime})

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\mathsf{unfold}(M)\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{unfold}(M^{\prime})

Figure 2.
Weak and Probabilistic reduction axioms and rules for

\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}

2.2.
Observational equivalence

In order to define observational equivalence, we need to represent the
probability of convergence of a term to a normal form. As
in
[
4
]
, we consider the reduction as a discrete time
Markov chain whose states are terms and stationary states are weak
normal terms. We then define a stochastic matrix

\mathsf{Red}\in[0,1]^{\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}\times\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}}

(indexed by terms) as

\mathsf{Red}_{M,M^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}p&\text{if }M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}\\
1&\text{if $M$ is weak-normal and $M^{\prime}=M$}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Saying that
\mathsf{Red}
is stochastic means that the coefficients of

\mathsf{Red}
belong to
[0,1]
and that, for any given term
M
, one
has
\sum_{M^{\prime}}\mathsf{Red}_{M,M^{\prime}}=1
(actually there are at most two terms

M^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{Red}_{M,M^{\prime}}\not=0
).

For all
M,M^{\prime}\in\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
, if
M^{\prime}
is weak-normal then the sequence

(\mathsf{Red}^{n}_{M,M^{\prime}})_{n=1}^{\infty}
is monotone and included in

[0,1]
, and therefore has a lub that we denote as

\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{M,M^{\prime}}
which defines a sub-stochastic matrix (taking

\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{M,M^{\prime}}=0
when
M^{\prime}
is not weak-normal).

When
M^{\prime}
is weak-normal, the number
p=\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{M,M^{\prime}}
is the
probability that
M
reduces to
M^{\prime}
after a finite number of steps.

Let us say when two closed terms
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
of type
\sigma
are

observationally equivalent
:

M_{1}\mathrel{\sim}M_{2}
, if for all closed term
C
of type

{\oc\sigma}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}
,
\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{\langle C\rangle M_{1}^{\mathord{\oc}},()}=\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{\langle C\rangle M_{1}^{\mathord{\oc}},()}
.

For simplicity we consider only closed terms
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
. We could also
define an observational equivalence on non closed terms, replacing the term
C

with a context
C[\ ]
which could bind free variables of the
M_{i}
’s, this
would not change the results of the paper.

2.3.
Examples

For the sake of readability, we drop the fold/unfold constructs
associated with recursive types definitions; they can easily be
inserted at the right places.
This also means that, in these examples, we consider the types

\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi
and
{\varphi}\left[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi}/{\zeta}\right]
as identical.

Ever-looping program.

Given any type
\sigma
, we define
\Omega^{\sigma}=\mathsf{fix}\,x^{\oc\sigma}\,\mathsf{der}\,x

which satisfies
\vdash\Omega^{\sigma}:\sigma
. It is clear that

\Omega^{\sigma}\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}\mathsf{der}\,(\Omega^{\sigma})^{\mathord{\oc}}\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}\Omega^{\sigma}
so
that we can consider
\Omega^{\sigma}
as the ever-looping program of type

\sigma
.

Booleans.

We define the type
o={\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}
, so that
\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{coin}(p):o
. We define the “true” constant as

\mathbf{t}=\mathsf{in}_{\ell}()
and the “false” constant as
\mathbf{f}=\mathsf{in}_{r}()
.
The corresponding eliminator is defined as follows.
Given terms
M
,
N_{\ell}
and
N_{r}

we set
\mathsf{if}(M,N_{\ell},N_{r})=\mathsf{case}(M,x_{\ell}\cdot N_{\ell},x_{r}\cdot N_{r})
where
x_{i}
is not free in
N_{i}
for
i\in\{\ell,r\}
, so that

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:o

\mathcal{P}\vdash N_{\ell}:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash N_{r}:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{if}(M,N_{\ell},N_{r}):\sigma

We have the following weak and probabilistic reduction rules, derived
from Figure
2
:

\mathsf{if}(\mathbf{t},N_{\ell},N_{r})\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}{N_{\ell}}

\mathsf{if}(\mathbf{f},N_{\ell},N_{r})\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}{N_{r}}

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\mathsf{if}(M,N_{\ell},N_{r})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{if}(M^{\prime},N_{\ell},N_{r})

Natural numbers.

We define the type
\iota
of unary natural numbers by

\iota={\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\iota}
(by this we mean that

\iota=\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot({\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\zeta})
). We define
\underline{0}=\mathsf{in}_{\ell}()

and
\underline{n+1}=\mathsf{in}_{r}\underline{n}
so that we have
\mathcal{P}\vdash\underline{n}:\iota
for each
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Then, given a term
M
, we define the term
\mathsf{suc}(M)=\mathsf{in}_{r}M
, so that
we have

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\iota

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{suc}(M):\iota

Last, given terms
M
,
N_{\ell}
and
N_{r}
and a
variable
x
, we define an “
ifz
” conditional by

\mathsf{ifz}(M,N_{\ell},x\cdot N_{r})=\mathsf{case}(M,z\cdot N_{\ell},x\cdot N_{r})
where
z
is not free
in
N_{\ell}
, so that

\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\iota

\mathcal{P}\vdash N_{\ell}:\sigma

\mathcal{P},x:\iota\vdash N_{r}:\sigma

\mathcal{P}\vdash\mathsf{ifz}(M,N_{\ell},x\cdot N_{r}):\sigma

We have the following weak and probabilistic reduction rules, derived
from Figure
2
:

i\in\{\ell,r\}

\mathsf{ifz}(\mathsf{in}_{i}V,M_{\ell},x\cdot M_{r})\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}{M_{i}}\left[{V}/{x}\right]

M\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}M^{\prime}

\mathsf{ifz}(M,N_{\ell},x\cdot N_{r})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\rightarrow}}}\mathsf{ifz}(M^{\prime},N_{\ell},x\cdot N_{r})

These conditionals will be used in the examples below.

Streams.

Let
\varphi
be a positive type and
\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}
be the positive type
defined by
\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}=\oc({\varphi}\otimes{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}})
, that is

\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}=\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\oc({\varphi}\otimes{\zeta})
. We can define a
term
M
such that
\vdash M:{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}\multimap{{\iota}\multimap{\varphi}}

which computes the
n
th element of a stream:

\displaystyle M=\mathsf{fix}\,f^{\oc({\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}\multimap{{\iota}\multimap{\varphi}})}\,\lambda x^{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}\,\lambda y^{\iota}\,{}\mathsf{ifz}(y,\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}(\mathsf{der}\,x),z\cdot\langle\mathsf{der}\,f\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{r}(\mathsf{der}\,x){\,z})

Let
O=(\Omega^{{\varphi}\otimes{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}})^{\mathord{\oc}}
, a term which represents the
“undefined stream” (more precisely, it is a stream which is a value, but
contains nothing, not to be confused with
\Omega^{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}
which has the
same type but is not a value). We have
{}\vdash{O}:{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}
, and observe
that the reduction of
\langle M\rangle O
converges (to an abstraction) and that

\langle M\rangle O\,\underline{0}
diverges.

Conversely, we can define a term
N
such that
\vdash N:{\oc({\iota}\multimap{\varphi})}\multimap{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}
which turns a
function into the stream of its successive applications to an integer.

\displaystyle N=\mathsf{fix}\,F^{\oc({\oc({\iota}\multimap{\varphi})}\multimap{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}})}\,\lambda f^{\oc({\iota}\multimap{\varphi})}\,\left(\langle\mathsf{der}\,f\rangle\underline{0},\langle\mathsf{der}\,F\rangle(\lambda x^{\iota}\,\langle\mathsf{der}\,f\rangle\mathsf{suc}(x))^{\mathord{\oc}}\right)^{\mathord{\oc}}

Observe that the recursive call of
F
is encapsulated into a box,
which makes the construction lazy.
As a last example, consider the following term
P
such that

{}\vdash{P}:{{({\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}\otimes{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}})}\multimap{{({\iota}\oplus{\iota})}\multimap{\varphi}}}

given by

\displaystyle P=\lambda y^{{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}\otimes{\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}}}\,\lambda c^{{\iota}\oplus{\iota}}\,\mathsf{case}(c,x\cdot\langle M\rangle x\,\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}y,x\cdot\langle M\rangle x\,\mathsf{pr}_{r}y)

Take
\varphi=\mathbf{1}
and consider the term
Q=\left(\left((),O\right)^{\mathord{\oc}},O\right)
,
then we have
{}\vdash{Q}:{{\mathsf{S}_{1}}\otimes{\mathsf{S}_{1}}}
, and observe that

\langle P\rangle Q\,{\mathsf{in}_{\ell}\underline{0}}
converges to
()
whereas
\langle P\rangle Q\,{\mathsf{in}_{r}\underline{0}}
diverges.

These examples suggest that
\mathsf{S}_{\varphi}
behaves as should behave a type of
streams of elements of type
\varphi
.

Lists.

There are various possibilities for defining a
type of lists of elements of a positive type
\varphi
. The simplest
definition is
\lambda_{0}={\mathbf{1}}\oplus{({\varphi}\otimes{\lambda_{0}})}
. This
corresponds to the ordinary ML type of lists. But we can also define

\lambda_{1}=\oc({\mathbf{1}}\oplus{{({\varphi}\otimes{\lambda_{1}})}})
and then we have
a type of lazy lists (or terminable streams) where the tail of the
list is computed only when required. Here is an example of a term
L

such that
{}\vdash{L}:{\lambda_{1}}
, with
\varphi=o={\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}

which is a list of random length containing random booleans:

\displaystyle L=\mathsf{fix}\,x^{\oc\lambda_{1}}\,(\mathsf{if}(\mathsf{coin}(1/4),\mathsf{in}_{\ell}(),\mathsf{in}_{r}{\left(\mathsf{coin}(1/2),\mathsf{der}\,x\right)}))^{\mathord{\oc}}

Then
\mathsf{der}\,L
will reduce with probability
\frac{1}{4}
to the empty list

\mathsf{in}_{\ell}()
, and with probability
\frac{3}{8}
to
each of the values

\mathsf{in}_{r}\left(\mathbf{t},L\right)
and
\mathsf{in}_{r}\left(\mathbf{f},L\right)
.

We can iterate this process, defining a term
R
of type

{\lambda_{1}}\multimap{\lambda_{0}}
which evaluates completely a terminable
stream to a list:

\displaystyle R=\mathsf{fix}\,f^{\oc({\lambda_{1}}\multimap{\lambda_{0}})}\,\lambda x^{\lambda_{1}}\,\mathsf{case}(\mathsf{der}\,x,z\cdot\mathsf{in}_{\ell}(),z\cdot{\left(\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}z,\langle\mathsf{der}\,f\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{r}z\right)})\,.

Then
\langle R\rangle L
, which is a closed term of type
\lambda_{0}
,
terminates with probability
1
. The expectation of the length of this
“random list” is
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n(\frac{3}{4})^{n}=12
.

Probabilistic tests.

If
\mathcal{P}\vdash M_{i}:\sigma
for
i=1,2
, we set

\mathsf{dice}_{p}(M_{1},M_{2})=\mathsf{if}(\mathsf{coin}(p),M_{1},M_{2})
and this term satisfies
{\mathcal{P}}\vdash{\mathsf{dice}_{p}(M_{1},M_{2})}:{\sigma}
. If

M_{i}
reduces to a value
V_{i}
with probability
q_{i}
, then

\mathsf{dice}_{p}(M_{1},M_{2})
reduces to
V_{1}
with probability
p\,q_{1}
and to
V_{2}
with probability
(1-p)q_{2}
.

Let
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and let
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern be such that

and
p_{0}+\cdots+p_{n}\leq 1
. Then one defines a
closed term

, which reduces to

with probability

for
each
i\in\{0,\dots,n\}
. The definition is by induction on
n
.

\mathsf{ran}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle p\hfil$\crcr}}})=\begin{cases}\underline{0}&\hskip-100.00015pt\text{if }p_{0}=1\text{ whatever be the value of }n\\
\mathsf{if}(\mathsf{coin}(p_{0}),\underline{0},\Omega^{\iota})&\hskip-100.00015pt\text{if }n=0\\
\mathsf{if}(\mathsf{coin}(p_{0}),\underline{0},\mathsf{suc}(\mathsf{ran}(\frac{p_{1}}{1-p_{0}},\dots,\frac{p_{n}}{1-p_{0}})))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

As an example of use of the test to zero conditional, we define, by induction on
k
, a family of terms
\mathsf{eq}_{k}
such that

{}\vdash{\mathsf{eq}_{k}}:{{\iota}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}}
and that tests the equality to
k
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{eq}_{0}=\lambda{x}^{\iota}\,{\mathsf{ifz}(x,(),z\cdot\Omega^{\mathbf{1}})}\qquad\mathsf{eq}_{k+1}=\lambda{x}^{\iota}\,{\mathsf{ifz}(x,\Omega^{\mathbf{1}},z\cdot\langle\mathsf{eq}_{k}\rangle z)}

For
M
such that
{}\vdash{M}:{\iota}
, the term
\langle\mathsf{eq}_{k}\rangle M
reduces to

()
with a probability which is equal to the probability of
M
to reduce
to
\underline{k}
.

2.3.1.
Notation.

Now, we introduce terms that will be used in the definition of testing terms
in the proof of Full Abstraction in Section
5
.

First, we define
\mathsf{prod}_{k}
such that

{}\vdash{\mathsf{prod}_{k}}:{{\mathbf{1}}\multimap{\cdots{}\multimap{{\mathbf{1}}\multimap{{\varphi}\multimap{\varphi}}}}}

(with
k
occurrences of
\mathbf{1}
):

\displaystyle\mathsf{prod}_{0}=\lambda y^{\varphi}\,y\qquad\mathsf{prod}_{k+1}=\lambda x^{\mathbf{1}}\,\mathsf{prod}_{k}\,.

Given for each
i\in\{0,\dots,k\}
,
M_{i}
such that
{\mathcal{P}}\vdash{M_{i}}:{\mathbf{1}}
and
{\mathcal{P}}\vdash{N}:{\varphi}
, the term

\langle\langle\langle\mathsf{prod}_{k+1}\rangle M_{0}\cdots\rangle M_{k}\rangle N
reduces to a value
V
with probability

p_{0}\,\cdots\,p_{k}\,q
where
p_{i}
is the probability of
M_{i}
to reduce to
()
and
q
is the probability of
N
to reduce to
V
.
We use the notations:

M_{0}\cdot N=\langle\langle\mathsf{prod}_{1}\rangle M_{0}\rangle N\qquad M_{0}\and\cdots\and M_{k-1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}()\text{ if $k=0$}\\
\langle\langle\mathsf{prod}_{k}\rangle M_{0}{\cdots}\rangle M_{k-1}\text{ otherwise},\end{array}\right.

so that

\mathcal{P}\vdash M_{0}\cdot N:\varphi
and the probability that
M_{0}\cdot N
reduces to
V
is
p_{0}\,q

and

\mathcal{P}\vdash M_{0}\and\cdots\and M_{k-1}:\mathbf{1}
and
M_{0}\and\cdots\and M_{k-1}
reduces to
()
with probability
p_{0}\,\cdots\,p_{k-1}
.

Given a general type
\sigma
and terms
M_{0},\dots,M_{k-1}
such that, for
any
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
,
{}\vdash{M_{i}}:{\sigma}
, we define close terms

\mathsf{choose}^{\sigma}_{i}(M_{0},\dots,M_{k-1})
for
i\in\{0,\dots,n-1\}
such
that
{}\vdash{\mathsf{choose}^{\sigma}_{i}(M_{0},\dots,M_{k-1})}:{{\iota}\multimap{{\sigma}}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{choose}^{\sigma}_{0}(M_{0},\dots,M_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\lambda{z}^{\iota}\,{\Omega^{\sigma}}

\displaystyle\mathsf{choose}^{\sigma}_{i+1}(M_{0},\dots,M_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\lambda z^{\iota}\,\mathsf{ifz}(z,M_{0},y\cdot\langle\mathsf{choose}^{\sigma}_{i}(M_{1},\dots,M_{k-1})\rangle y)\text{ if }i\leq k-1

Given a term
P
such that
{\mathcal{P}}\vdash{P}:{\iota}
and
p_{i}
the
probability of
P
to reduce to
\underline{i}
for any
i
, the
first steps of the reduction are probabilistic:

\displaystyle\forall i\in\{0,\dots,k\},\ {\langle\mathsf{choose}^{\sigma}_{k+1}(M_{0},\dots,M_{k})\rangle P}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{i}}}{{\rightarrow^{*}}}}{\langle\mathsf{choose}^{\varphi}_{k+1}(M_{0},\dots,M_{k})\rangle\underline{i}}

the next steps of the reduction are deterministic:

\displaystyle\langle\mathsf{choose}^{\varphi}_{k+1}(M_{0},\dots,M_{k})\rangle\underline{i}\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}^{*}}M_{i}

As we will see more precisely in
Paragraph
3.2.12
, a term of type
\iota
can be
seen as a sub-probability distribution over
{\mathbb{N}}
. Given integers

0\leq l\leq r
, we define by induction the term
\mathsf{ext}\left(l,\,r\right)
of type

{\iota}\multimap{\iota}
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{ext}\left(0,\,0\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda z^{\iota}\,\mathsf{ifz}(z,\underline{0},x\cdot\Omega^{\iota})

\displaystyle\forall r>0,\ \mathsf{ext}\left(0,\,r\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{choose}^{\iota}_{r+1}{(\underline{0},\dots,\underline{r})}

\displaystyle\mathsf{ext}\left(l+1,\,r+1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda z^{\iota}\,\mathsf{ifz}(z,\Omega^{\iota},x\cdot\mathsf{suc}(\langle\mathsf{ext}\left(l,\,r\right)\rangle x))

such that if
{}\vdash{P}:{\iota}
, then
\langle\mathsf{ext}\left(l,\,r\right)\rangle P
extracts the
sub-probability distribution with support

\subseteq\{l,\dots,r\}
. Indeed, for any
i\in\{l,\dots,r\}

\langle\mathsf{ext}\left(l,\,r\right)\rangle P
reduces to

\underline{i}
with probability
p_{i}
where
p_{i}
is the probability of
P
to reduce to
\underline{i}
.

We also introduce, for
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern :

2.4.
On products and recursive definitions of general types

This section has nothing to do with probabilities, so we consider the
deterministic language
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
, which is just
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
without the

\mathsf{coin}(p)
construct. Our
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
general types, which are similar to
Levy’s CBPV computation types
[
22
]
or to the SFPL general
types
[
23
]
, have
{}\multimap{}
as only type
constructor. This may seem weird when one
compares our language with CBPV where products and recursive
definitions are possible on computation types and are used for
encoding CBN functional languages such as
\mathsf{PCF}
extended with
products.

For keeping our presentation reasonably short, we will not consider
the corresponding extensions of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
in this paper. Instead, we
will shortly argue that such a
\mathsf{PCF}
language with products can be
easily encoded in our
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
.

Concerning recursive type definitions, it is true that adding them as
well as the cartesian product
\mathrel{\&}
at the level of general types
would allow to define interesting types such as

\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot{\mathbf{1}}\mathrel{\&}{({\oc\zeta}\multimap{\zeta})}
, yielding a
straightforward encoding of the pure lambda-calculus in our
language. This goal can nevertheless be reached (admittedly in a
slightly less natural way) by using the positive recursive type
definition
\varphi=\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\oc({\zeta}\multimap{\zeta})
. A pure
term
t
will be translated into a
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
term
{t}^{*}
such that

{x_{1}:{\varphi_{1}},\dots,x_{n}:\varphi}\vdash{{t}^{*}}:{{\varphi}\multimap{\varphi}}
, where
the list
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
contains all the free variables of
t
. This
translation is defined inductively as follows:
{x}^{*}=\mathsf{der}\,x
,

{(\left({s}\right){t})}^{*}=\mathsf{der}\,(\langle{s}^{*}\rangle({t}^{*})^{\mathord{\oc}})
and

{(\lambda{x}\,{s})}^{*}=\lambda{x}\,{({s}^{*})^{\mathord{\oc}}}
. A simple computation
shows that
\beta
-reduction is validated by this interpretation, but
observe that it is not the case for
\eta
. The examples we provide
in Section
2.3
also show that our recursive definitions
restricted to positive types allow to introduce a lot of useful data
types (integers, lists, trees, streams etc). So we do not see any real
motivations for adding recursive general type definitions (and their
addition would make the proof of adequacy in
Section
4
even more complicated).

Coming back to the encoding of products, consider the
following grammar of types

\displaystyle A,B,\dots\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\mathsf{nat}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,{A}\Rightarrow{B}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,A\times B

and the following language of terms

\displaystyle s,t,u,\dots\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\underline{n}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{suc}\,s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{pred}\,s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{ifz}(s,t,u)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\lambda{x}^{A}\,{s}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\left({s}\right){t}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,(s,t)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}\,s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{pr}_{r}\,s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{fix}\,x^{A}\cdot s

We call this language
\mathsf{PCF}
as it is a straightforward extension of the
original
\mathsf{PCF}
of
[
27
]
.

The typing rules are described in Figure
3
. A typing
context is a sequence
\Gamma=(x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:A_{n})
where the
variables are pairwise distinct.

We explain now how we interpret this simple programming language in

\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}
.

Translating
\mathsf{PCF}
types.

With any type
A
, we associate a
finite sequence
of general
types
{A}^{*}=({A}^{*}_{1},\dots,{A}^{*}_{n})
whose length

n=\mathsf{l}(A)
is given by:
\mathsf{l}(\mathsf{nat})=1
,
\mathsf{l}({A}\Rightarrow{B})=\mathsf{l}(B)
and

\mathsf{l}(A\times B)=\mathsf{l}(A)+\mathsf{l}(B)
.

Given a sequence
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\sigma\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern .

Using these notations, we can now define
{A}^{*}
as follows:

•

{\mathsf{nat}}^{*}=(\iota)
(a one element sequence) where
\iota
is
the type of integers introduced in Section
2.3
,

\iota=\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot({\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\zeta})
,

•

{({A}\Rightarrow{B})}^{*}={\oc{A}^{*}}\multimap{{B}^{*}}
,

•

{(A\times B)}^{*}={A}^{*}\cdot{B}^{*}
(list concatenation).

Translating
\mathsf{PCF}
terms.

Let now
s
be a
\mathsf{PCF}
term with typing judgment
7
7

7

So our
translation depends on the typing judgment and not only on the term;
this is fairly standard and can be avoided by considering typed free
variables.

{\Gamma}\vdash{s}:{A}
. Let
n=\mathsf{l}(A)
, we define a sequence

{s}^{*}
of length
n
such that

{\oc\Gamma}\vdash{{s}^{*}_{i}}:{{A}^{*}_{i}}
(for
i=1,\dots,n
) as
follows (if
\Gamma=(x_{1}:C_{1},\dots,x_{k}:C_{k})
, then

\oc\Gamma=(x_{1}:\oc{C_{1}}^{*},\dots,x_{k}:\oc{C_{k}}^{*})
).

If
s=x_{j}
, so that
A=C_{j}
(for some
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
), let

(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{n})={A}^{*}
. Since

\oc{A}^{*}={\oc\sigma_{1}}\otimes{({\oc\sigma_{2}}\otimes{\cdots({\oc\sigma_{n-1}}\otimes{({\oc\sigma_{n}}\otimes{\mathbf{1}})})\cdots})}

we can set
{x}^{*}=({x}^{*}_{1},\dots,{x}^{*}_{n})
where

{x}^{*}_{i}=\mathsf{der}\,\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}\mathsf{pr}_{r}\mathsf{pr}_{r}\cdots\mathsf{pr}_{r}x
(with
i-1
occurrences of

\mathsf{pr}_{r}
).

We set
{\underline{n}}^{*}=\mathsf{in}_{r}\cdots\mathsf{in}_{r}\mathsf{in}_{\ell}()
(
n
occurrences of

\mathsf{in}_{r}
),
{(\mathsf{suc}\,s)}^{*}=\mathsf{in}_{r}{s}^{*}
,

{(\mathsf{pred}\,s)}^{*}=\mathsf{case}({s}^{*},x\cdot\mathsf{in}_{\ell}(),x\cdot x)
. Assume that

s=\mathsf{ifz}(t,u,v)
with
{\Gamma}\vdash{t}:{\iota}
,
{\Gamma}\vdash{u}:{A}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{v}:{A}

for some
\mathsf{PCF}
type
A
. Let
l=\mathsf{l}(A)
. By inductive hypothesis we have

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{{t}^{*}}:{\iota}
,

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{{u}^{*}_{i}}:{{A}^{*}_{i}}
and

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{{v}^{*}_{i}}:{{A}^{*}_{i}}
for
i=1,\dots,l
. So we
set
{s}^{*}=(\mathsf{case}({t}^{*},z\cdot{u}^{*}_{i},z\cdot{v}^{*}_{i}))_{i=1}^{l}
where

z
is a variable which does not occur free in
u
or
v
.

Assume now that
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{s}:{B}
, we set

{(\lambda{x}^{A}\,{s})}^{*}=(\lambda x^{\oc{A}^{*}}\,{s}^{*}_{i})_{i=1}^{\mathsf{l}(B)}
.
Assume that
{\Gamma}\vdash{s}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{t}:{A}
. Then, setting

n=\mathsf{l}(B)
, we have

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{{s}^{*}_{i}}:{{\oc{A}^{*}}\multimap{{B}^{*}_{i}}}

for
i=1,\dots,n
and
{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{{t}^{*}_{j}}:{{A}^{*}_{j}}
for

j=1,\dots,m
where
m=\mathsf{l}(A)
. Then, setting

N=\left(({t_{1}}^{*})^{\mathord{\oc}},\left(\cdots,\left(({t_{m-1}}^{*})^{\mathord{\oc}},\left(({t_{m}}^{*})^{\mathord{\oc}},()\right)\right)\cdots\right)\right)

we have
{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{N}:{\oc{A}^{*}}
and we set

{(\left({s}\right){t})}^{*}=(\langle{s}^{*}_{i}\rangle N)_{i=1}^{n}
. Assume that

s=(s_{1},s_{2})
with
{\Gamma}\vdash{s_{i}}:{A_{i}}
for
i=1,2
and

{\Gamma}\vdash{s}:{A_{1}\times A_{2}}
. Then we set

{s}^{*}={s_{1}}^{*}\cdot{s_{2}}^{*}
(list concatenation). Assume that

{\Gamma}\vdash{s}:{A_{1}\times A_{2}}
, with
n_{i}=\mathsf{l}(A_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Then we
set
{(\mathsf{pr}_{\ell}\,s)}^{*}=({s}^{*}_{1},\dots,{s}^{*}_{n_{1}})
and

{(\mathsf{pr}_{r}\,s)}^{*}=({s}^{*}_{n_{1}+1},\dots,{s}^{*}_{n_{1}+n_{2}})
.

Last assume that
s=\mathsf{fix}\,x^{A}\cdot t
with
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{t}:{A}
so that, setting

n=\mathsf{l}(A)
, we have

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*},x:\oc{A}^{*}}\vdash{{t}^{*}_{i}}:{{A}^{*}_{i}}
for

i=1,\dots,n
. Let
x_{1},\dots,x_{n}
be pairwise distinct fresh variables, and set

M_{i}={{t}^{*}_{i}}\left[{\left({x_{1}},\cdots\left({x_{n-1}},{\left(x_{n},()\right)}\right)\cdots\right)}/{x}\right]

for
i=1,\dots,n
. We have

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*},x_{1}:\oc{A}^{*}_{1},\dots,x_{n}:\oc{A}^{*}_{n}}\vdash{M_{i}}:{{A}^{*}_{i}}
.
We are in position of applying the usual trick for encoding mutual
recursive definitions using fixpoints operators.
For the sake of readability, assume that
n=2
, so we have

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*},x_{1}:\oc{A}^{*}_{1},x_{2}:\oc{A}^{*}_{2}}\vdash{M_{i}}:{{A}^{*}_{i}}
for
i=1,2
.
Let
N_{1}=\mathsf{fix}\,x_{1}^{\oc{A}^{*}_{1}}\,M_{1}
so that

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*},x_{2}:\oc{A}^{*}_{2}}\vdash{N_{1}}:{{A}^{*}_{1}}
.
Then we have

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*},x_{2}:\oc{A}^{*}_{2}}\vdash{{M_{2}}\left[{N_{1}^{\mathord{\oc}}}/{x_{1}}\right]}:{{A}^{*}_{2}}
.
Therefore we can set

{s}^{*}_{2}=\mathsf{fix}\,x_{2}^{\oc{A}^{*}_{2}}\,{M_{2}}\left[{N_{1}^{\mathord{\oc}}}/{x_{1}}\right]
and
we have
{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{{s}^{*}_{2}}:{{A}^{*}_{2}}
. Finally
we set
{s}^{*}_{1}={N_{1}}\left[{({s}^{*}_{2})^{\mathord{\oc}}}/{x_{2}}\right]
with

{\oc{\Gamma}^{*}}\vdash{{s}^{*}_{1}}:{{A}^{*}_{1}}
.

We should check now that this translation is compatible with the
operational semantics of our extended
\mathsf{PCF}
language. A simple way to do
so would be to choose a simple model of linear logic (for instance,
the relational model) and to prove that the semantics of a
\mathsf{PCF}
term is
equal to the semantics of its translation in
\Lambda_{\mathsf{HP}}^{\mathsf{p}}
stripped from
its probabilistic construct
\mathsf{coin}(p)
(interpreting tuples of types
using the “additive” cartesian product
\mathrel{\&}
). This is a long and
boring exercise.

{\Gamma}\vdash{s}:{\mathsf{nat}}

Typing rules for a simple call-by-name language with products, PCF

3.
Probabilistic Coherent Spaces

3.1.
Semantics of LL, in a nutshell

The kind of denotational models we are interested in, in this paper, are those
induced by a model of LL, as explained in
[
5
]
. We remind the basic
definitions and notations, referring to that paper for more details.

3.1.1.
Models of Linear Logic.

A model of LL consists of the following data.

A symmetric monoidal closed category

(\mathcal{L},\otimes,1,\lambda,\rho,\alpha,\sigma)
where we use simple
juxtaposition
g\,f
to denote composition of morphisms
f\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
and

g\in\mathcal{L}(Y,Z)
. We use
{X}\multimap{Y}
for the object of linear morphisms from
X

to
Y
,
\operatorname{\mathrm{ev}}\in\mathcal{L}({({X}\multimap{Y})}\otimes{X},Y)
for the evaluation morphism and

\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}\in\mathcal{L}({Z}\otimes{X},Y)\to\mathcal{L}(Z,{X}\multimap{Y})
for the linear curryfication
map. For convenience, and because it is the case in the concrete models we
consider (such as Scott Semantics
[
5
]
or Probabilistic Coherent
Spaces here), we assume this SMCC to be a
*
-autonomous category with
dualizing object
{\mathord{\perp}}
. We use
X^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
for the object
{X}\multimap{{\mathord{\perp}}}
of

\mathcal{L}
(the dual, or linear negation, of
X
).

The category
\mathcal{L}
is cartesian with terminal object
\top
, product

\mathrel{\&}
, projections
{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}
. By
*
-autonomy
\mathcal{L}
is co-cartesian
with initial object
0
, coproduct
\oplus
and injections

{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
. By monoidal closeness of
\mathcal{L}
, the tensor product
\otimes

distributes over the coproduct
\oplus
.

We are given a comonad
\oc{\_}:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
with co-unit
\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{X},X)
(
dereliction
) and co-multiplication
\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{X},\oc{\oc{X}})
(
digging
) together with a strong symmetric monoidal
structure (Seely isos
\mathrm{m}^{0}
and
\mathrm{m}^{2}
) for the functor
\oc{\_}
, from
the symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{L},\mathrel{\&})
to the symmetric monoidal
category
(\mathcal{L},\otimes)
satisfying an additional coherence condition
wrt.
\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}
.

We use
\wn{\_}
for the “De Morgan dual” of
\oc{\_}
:
\wn{X}=(\oc({X^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}))^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
and similarly for morphisms. It is a monad on
\mathcal{L}
.

3.1.2.
The Eilenberg-Moore category.

It is then standard to define the category
\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}
of
{\mathord{!}}
-coalgebras. An
object of this category is a pair
P=(\underline{P},\mathrm{h}_{P})
where
\underline{P}\in\mathsf{Obj}(\mathcal{L})
and
\mathrm{h}_{P}\in\mathcal{L}(\underline{P},\oc{\underline{P}})
is such that

\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{\underline{P}}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}=\operatorname{\mathrm{Id}}
and
\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{\underline{P}}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}=\oc{\mathrm{h}_{P}}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}
. Then
f\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}(P,Q)
iff

f\in\mathcal{L}(\underline{P},\underline{Q})
such that
\mathrm{h}_{Q}\,f=\oc{f}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}
. The functor
\oc{\_}
can be seen as a functor from
\mathcal{L}

to
\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}
mapping
X
to
(\oc{X},\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X})
and
f\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
to
\oc{f}
. It is right adjoint to the forgetful functor

\mathsf{U}:\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}\to\mathcal{L}
. Given
f\in\mathcal{L}(\underline{P},X)
, we use
f^{!}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}(P,\oc{X})
for the morphism associated with
f
by this adjunction,
one has
f^{!}=\oc{f}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}
. If
g\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}(Q,P)
, we have

f^{!}\,g=\left(f\,g\right)^{!}
.

Then
\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}
is cartesian (with product of shape
{P}\otimes{Q}=({\underline{P}}\otimes{\underline{Q}},\mathrm{h}_{{P}\otimes{Q}})
and terminal object
(1,\mathrm{h}_{1})
,
still denoted as
1
). This category is also co-cartesian with coproduct of
shape
{P}\oplus{Q}=({\underline{P}}\oplus{\underline{Q}},\mathrm{h}_{{P}\oplus{Q}})
and initial
object
(0,\mathrm{h}_{0})
still denoted as
0
. The complete
definitions can be found in
[
5
]
. We use
\operatorname{\mathrm{c}}_{P}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}(P,{P}\otimes{P})
(
contraction
) for the diagonal and
\operatorname{\mathrm{w}}_{P}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}(P,1)

(
weakening
) for the unique morphism to the terminal object.

We also consider occasionally the
Kleisli category
8
8

8

It is the
full subcategory of
\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}
of free coalgebras, see any introductory text on
monads and co-monads.

{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
of the
comonad
\oc
: its objects are those of
\mathcal{L}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,Y)=\mathcal{L}(\oc{X},Y)
. The identity at
X
in this category is
\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}
and composition of

f\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
and
g\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(Y,Z)
is defined as

\displaystyle g\mathrel{\circ}f=g\,\oc{f}\,\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X}\,.

This category is cartesian closed but this fact will not play an essential role
in this work.

3.1.3.
Fixpoints.

For any object
X
, we assume to be given
{\mathrm{fix}}_{X}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc({{\oc{X}}\multimap{X}}),X)
, a morphism such that
9
9

9

It might seem natural to require
the stronger uniformity conditions of
Conway
operator

[
30
]
. This does not seem to be necessary as
far as soundness of our semantics is concerned even if the fixpoint
operators arising in concrete models satisfy these further properties.

\operatorname{\mathrm{ev}}\,({\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{{\oc{X}}\multimap{X}}}\otimes{{\mathrm{fix}}_{X}^{!}})\mathrel{\circ}\operatorname{\mathrm{c}}_{\oc{({\oc{X}}\multimap{X})}}={\mathrm{fix}}_{X}
which will allow to interpret
term fixpoints.

In order to interpret fixpoints of types, we assume that the category
\mathcal{L}
is
equipped with a notion of embedding-retraction pairs, following a standard
approach. We use
\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
for the corresponding category. It is
equipped with a functor
\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}\to{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathsf{op}}\times\mathcal{L}
such that

\mathsf{F}(X)=(X,X)
and for which we use the notation

(\varphi^{-},\varphi^{+})=\mathsf{F}(\varphi)
and assume that

\varphi^{-}\,\varphi^{+}=\operatorname{\mathrm{Id}}_{X}
. We assume furthermore that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
has all
countable directed colimits and that the functor

\mathsf{E}={\mathrm{pr}}_{2}\,\mathsf{F}:\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}\to\mathcal{L}
is continuous. We also assume that
all the basic operations on objects (
\otimes
,
\oplus
,
(\_)^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
and

\oc{\_}
) are continuous functors from
\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
to itself
10
10

10

This is a
rough statement; one has to say for instance that if

\varphi_{i}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}(X_{i},Y_{i})
for
i=1,2
then

({\varphi_{1}}\otimes{\varphi_{2}})^{-}={\varphi_{1}^{-}}\otimes{\varphi_{2}^{-}}
etc. The
details can be found in
[
5
]
.
.

Then it is easy to carry this notion of embedding-retraction pairs to
\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}
,
defining a category
\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
, to show that this category has all
countable directed colimits and that the functors
\otimes
and
\oplus
are
continuous on this category:
\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}(P,Q)
is the set of all

\varphi\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}(\underline{P},\underline{Q})
such that
\varphi^{+}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}(P,Q)
. One
checks also that
\oc{}
defines a continuous functor from
\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
to

\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
. This allows to interpret recursive types, more details can be
found in
[
5
]
.

Using straightforwardly the object
1
and the operations
\otimes
,

\oplus
,
\oc{}
and
{}\multimap{}
of the model
\mathcal{L}
as well as the
completeness and continuity properties explained in
Section
3.1.3
, we associate with any positive type
\varphi

and any repetition-free list

of pairwise
distinct type variables containing all free variables of
\sigma
we associate
a continuous functor
[{\sigma}]_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern
