2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}}:(\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}})^{n}\to\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
.

When we write
[{\sigma}]
or
[{\varphi}]^{\oc}
(without subscript), we assume
implicitly that the types
\sigma
and
\varphi
have no free type variables. Then

[{\sigma}]
is an object of
\mathcal{L}
and
[{\varphi}]^{\oc}
is an object of

\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}
. We have

[{\varphi}]=\underline{[{\varphi}]^{\oc}}
that is, considered as a generalized type, the
semantics of a positive type
\varphi
is the carrier of the coalgebra

[{\varphi}]^{\oc}
.

Given a typing context
\mathcal{P}=(x_{1}:\varphi_{1},\dots,x_{k}:\varphi_{k})
, we define

[{\mathcal{P}}]=[{\varphi_{1}}]^{\oc}\otimes\cdots\otimes[{\varphi_{k}}]^{\oc}\in\mathcal{L}^{\mathord{\oc}}
.

In the model or probabilistic coherence spaces considered in this paper, we
define
\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
in such a way that the only isos are the identity maps. This
implies that the types
\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi
and
{\varphi}\left[{(\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi)}/{\zeta}\right]

are interpreted as
the same object
(or functor). Such definitions of

\mathcal{L}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
are possible in many other models (relational, coherence spaces,
hypercoherences etc).

We postpone the description of term interpretation because this will
require constructions specific to our probabilistic semantics, in addition to
the generic categorical ingredients introduced so far.

3.2.
The model of probabilistic coherence spaces

Given a countable set
I
and
u,u^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{I}
, we set

\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{i\in I}u_{i}u^{\prime}_{i}
. Given
\mathcal{F}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{I}
, we set

\mathcal{F}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}=\{u^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{I}\mid\forall u\in\mathcal{F}\ \langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle\leq 1\}
.

A
probabilistic coherence space
(PCS) is a pair
X=(|{X}|,\mathsf{P}{X})

where
|{X}|
is a countable set and
\mathsf{P}{X}\subseteq(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X}|}

satisfies

•

\mathsf{P}{X}^{{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}}=\mathsf{P}{X}
(equivalently,
\mathsf{P}{X}^{{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}}\subseteq\mathsf{P}{X}
),

•

for each
a\in|{X}|
there exists
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
such that
u_{a}>0
,

•

for each
a\in|{X}|
there exists
A>0
such that
\forall u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ u_{a}\leq A
.

If only the first of these conditions holds, we say that
X
is a

pre-probabilistic coherence space
(pre-PCS).

The purpose of the second and third conditions is to prevent infinite
coefficients to appear in the semantics. This property in turn will be
essential for guaranteeing the morphisms interpreting proofs to be analytic
functions, which will be the key property to prove full abstraction. So these
conditions, though aesthetic at first sight, are important for our ultimate
goal.

Lemma 1
.

Let
X
be a pre-PCS. The following conditions are
equivalent:

•

X
is a PCS,

•

\forall a\in|{X}|\,\exists u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\,\exists u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{X}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}\ u_{a}>0\text{ and }u^{\prime}_{a}>0
,

•

\forall a\in|{X}|\,\exists A>0\,\forall u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\,\forall u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{X}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}\ u_{a}\leq A\text{ and }u^{\prime}_{a}\leq A
.

The proof is straightforward.

We equip
\mathsf{P}{X}
with the most obvious partial order relation:
u\leq v
if

\forall a\in|{X}|\ u_{a}\leq v_{a}
(using the usual order relation on
\mathbb{R}
).

Theorem 2
.

\mathsf{P}{X}
is an
\omega
-continuous domain. Given
u,v\in\mathsf{P}{X}
and

\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\alpha+\beta\leq 1
, one has
\alpha u+\beta v\in\mathsf{P}{X}
.

This is an easy consequence of the hypothesis

\mathsf{P}{X}^{{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}}\subseteq\mathsf{P}{X}
. See
[
4
]
for
details; from this result, we will only use the closure properties:

\mathsf{P}{X}
is closed under sub-probabilistic linear combinations and
under lubs of monotonic sequences. Though the
\omega
-continuity
property (and the associated way-below relation) does not play any
technical role, it is an intuitively satisfactory
fact
11
11

11

The
\omega
-continuity is similar to separability for topological vector spaces.

which means that the “size” of our domains remains bounded.

3.2.1.
Morphisms of PCSs

Let
X
and
Y
be PCSs. Let
t\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X}|\times|{Y}|}
(to be
understood as a matrix). Given
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, we define
{t}\,{u}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}^{+}}^{|{Y}|}
by
({t}\,{u})_{b}=\sum_{a\in|{X}|}t_{a,b}u_{a}

(application of the matrix
t
to the vector
u
)
12
12

12

This is an unordered
sum, which is infinite in general. It makes sense because all its terms are

\geq 0
.
. We say that
t
is a
(linear) morphism
from
X
to
Y
if

\forall u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ {t}\,{u}\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
, that is

\displaystyle\forall u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\,\forall{v^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{P}{Y}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}\quad\sum_{(a,b)\in|{X}|\times|{Y}|}t_{a,b}u_{a}v^{\prime}_{b}\leq 1\,.

The diagonal matrix
\operatorname{\mathrm{Id}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X}|\times|{X}|}
given by

\operatorname{\mathrm{Id}}_{a,b}=1
if
a=b
and
\operatorname{\mathrm{Id}}_{a,b}=0
otherwise is a morphism. In that way
we have defined a category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
whose objects are the PCSs and whose
morphisms have just been defined. Composition of morphisms is defined as matrix
multiplication: let
s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
and
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Y,Z)
, we define
{t}\,{s}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X}|\times|{Z}|}
by

\displaystyle({t}\,{s})_{a,c}=\sum_{b\in|{Y}|}s_{a,b}t_{b,c}

and a simple computation shows that
{t}\,{s}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Z)
. More precisely,
we use the fact that, given
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, one has
{({t}\,{s})}\,{u}={t}\,{({s}\,{u})}
. Associativity of composition holds because
matrix multiplication is associative.
\operatorname{\mathrm{Id}}_{X}
is the identity morphism at
X
.

Given
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, we define
\|{u}\|_{X}=\sup\{\langle u,u^{\prime}\rangle\mid u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{X^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}\}
. By definition, we have
\|{u}\|_{X}\in[0,1]
.

3.2.2.
Multiplicative constructs

One sets
X^{{\mathord{\perp}}}=(|{X}|,\mathsf{P}{X}^{{\mathord{\perp}}})
. It results straightforwardly from
the definition of PCSs that
X^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
is a PCS. Given
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
, one has

t^{{\mathord{\perp}}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}},X^{{\mathord{\perp}}})
if
t^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
is the transpose of
t
, that
is
(t^{{\mathord{\perp}}})_{b,a}=t_{a,b}
.

One defines
{X}\otimes{Y}
by

|{{X}\otimes{Y}}|=|{X}|\times|{Y}|
and

\mathsf{P}({X}\otimes{Y})=\{{u}\otimes{v}\mid u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\text{ and }v\in\mathsf{P}{Y}\}^{{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}}

where
\left({u}\otimes{v}\right)_{(a,b)}=u_{a}v_{b}
. Then
{X}\otimes{Y}
is a pre-PCS.

We have

\mathsf{P}{({X}\otimes{Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}=\{{u}\otimes{v^{\prime}}\mid u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\text{ and
}v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}\}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}=\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)\,.

It follows that
{X}\multimap{Y}=({X}\otimes{Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
is a pre-PCS. Let

(a,b)\in|{X}|\times|{Y}|
. Since
X
and
Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
are PCSs, there is
A>0

such that
u_{a}v^{\prime}_{b}<A
for all
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
and
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}
. Let

t\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{X}\multimap{Y}}|}
be such that
t_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}=0
for

(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\not=(a,b)
and
t_{(a,b)}=1/A
, we have
t\in\mathsf{P}{({X}\multimap{Y})}
. This
shows that
\exists t\in\mathsf{P}{({X}\multimap{Y})}
such that
t_{(a,b)}>0
. Similarly
we can find
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
and
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}
such that

\varepsilon=u_{a}v^{\prime}_{b}>0
. It follows that
\forall t\in\mathsf{P}{({X}\multimap{Y})}
one has

t_{(a,b)}\leq 1/\varepsilon
. We conclude that
{X}\multimap{Y}
is a PCS, and
therefore
{X}\otimes{Y}
is also a PCS.

Lemma 3
.

Let
X
and
Y
be PCSs. One has
\mathsf{P}{({X}\multimap{Y})}=\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
. That is,
given
t\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X}|\times|{Y}|}
, one has
t\in\mathsf{P}{({X}\multimap{Y})}

iff for all
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, one has
t\,u\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
.

This results immediately from the definition above of
{X}\multimap{Y}
.

Lemma 4
.

Let
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
and
Y
be PCSs. Let

t\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{{X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}}}\multimap{Y}}|}
. One has

t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}},Y)
iff for all
u_{1}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{1}}
and

u_{2}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{2}}
one has
{t}\,{({u_{1}}\otimes{u_{2}})}\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
.

Proof.

The condition stated by the lemma is clearly necessary. Let us prove that it is
sufficient: under this condition, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle t^{{\mathord{\perp}}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}},({X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}})^{{\mathord{\perp}}})\,.

Let
v^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}
, it suffices to prove that
{t^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}\,{v^{\prime}}\in\mathsf{P}{({X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}
. So let
u_{1}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{1}}
and

u_{2}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{2}}
, it suffices to prove that
\langle{t^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}\,{v^{\prime}},{u_{1}}\otimes{u_{2}}\rangle\leq 1
, that is
\langle{t}\,{({u_{1}}\otimes{u_{2}})},v^{\prime}\rangle\leq 1
, which follows from our assumption.

\Box

Let
s_{i}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X_{i},Y_{i})
for
i=1,2
. Then one defines

\displaystyle{s_{1}}\otimes{s_{2}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{{X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}}}\multimap{{Y_{1}}\otimes{Y_{2}}}}|}

by

({s_{1}}\otimes{s_{2}})_{((a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1},b_{2}))}=(s_{1})_{(a_{1},b_{1})}(s_{2})_{(a_{2},b_{2})}

and one must check that

{s_{1}}\otimes{s_{2}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({{X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}},{Y_{1}}\otimes{Y_{2}}})\,.

This follows directly from Lemma
4
. Let

1=(\{*\},[0,1])
. There are obvious choices of natural isomorphisms

\displaystyle\lambda_{X}

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({1}\otimes{X},X)

\displaystyle\rho_{X}

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({X}\otimes{1},X)

\displaystyle\alpha_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\left({X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}}\right)}\otimes{X_{3}},{X_{1}}\otimes{\left({X_{2}}\otimes{X_{3}}\right)})

\displaystyle\sigma_{X_{1},X_{2}}

\displaystyle\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}},{X_{2}}\otimes{X_{1}})

which satisfy the standard coherence properties. This shows that

(\mathbf{Pcoh},1,\lambda,\rho,\alpha,\sigma)
is a symmetric monoidal category.

3.2.3.
Internal linear hom.

Given PCSs
X
and
Y
, let us define

\operatorname{\mathrm{ev}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{{({X}\multimap{Y})}\otimes{X}}\multimap{Y}}|}
by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathrm{ev}}_{(((a^{\prime},b^{\prime}),a),b)}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }(a,b)=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then it is easy to see that
({X}\multimap{Y},\operatorname{\mathrm{ev}})
is an internal linear hom
object in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
, showing that this SMCC is closed. If
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({Z}\otimes{X},Y)
, the corresponding linearly curryfied morphism

\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}(t)\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Z,{X}\multimap{Y})
is given by

\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}(t)_{(c,(a,b))}=t_{((c,a),b)}
.

3.2.4.
*
-autonomy.

Take
{\mathord{\perp}}=1
, then one checks readily that

(\mathbf{Pcoh},1,\lambda,\rho,\alpha,\sigma,{\mathord{\perp}})
is a
*
-autonomous category. The
duality functor
X\mapsto({X}\multimap{{\mathord{\perp}}})
can be identified with the strictly
involutive contravariant functor
X\mapsto X^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
.

3.2.5.
Additives

Let
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
be a countable family of PCSs. We define a PCS

\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i}
by
|{\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i}}|=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{i\}\times|{X_{i}}|
and
u\in\mathsf{P}(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i})
if, for all

i\in I
, the family
u(i)\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X_{i}}|}
defined by

u(i)_{a}=u_{(i,a)}
belongs to
\mathsf{P}{X_{i}}
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
u^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i}}|}
. One has

u^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}
iff

•

\forall i\in I\ u^{\prime}(i)\in\mathsf{P}{X_{i}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}

•

and
\sum_{i\in I}\|{u^{\prime}(i)}\|_{X_{i}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}\leq 1
.

The proof is quite easy. It follows that
\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i}
is a
PCS. Moreover we can define
{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathop{\&}_{j\in I}X_{j},X_{i})
by

\displaystyle({\mathrm{pr}}_{i})_{(j,a),a^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }j=i\text{ and }a=a^{\prime}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i},({\mathrm{pr}}_{i})_{i\in I})
is the cartesian product of the
family
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
in the category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
. The coproduct
(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i},({\mathrm{in}}_{i})_{i\in I})
is the dual operation, so that

\displaystyle|{\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i}}|=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{i\}\times|{X_{i}}|

and
u\in\mathsf{P}{(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i})}
if
\forall i\in I\ u(i)\in\mathsf{P}{X_{i}}

and
\sum_{i\in I}\|{u(i)}\|_{X_{i}}\leq 1
. The injections

{\mathrm{in}}_{j}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X_{j},\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i})
are given by

\displaystyle({\mathrm{in}}_{i})_{a^{\prime},(j,a)}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }j=i\text{ and }a=a^{\prime}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Given morphisms
s_{i}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X_{i},Y)
(for each
i\in I
), then the
unique morphism
s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i},Y)
is given by

s_{(i,a),b}=(s_{i})_{a,b}
and denoted as
\mathrm{case}_{i\in I}s_{i}
(in the binary
case, we use
\mathrm{case}(s_{1},s_{2})
).

3.2.6.
Exponentials

Given a set
I
, a
finite multiset
of elements of
I
is a function

b:I\to{\mathbb{N}}
whose
support

\operatorname{\mathsf{supp}}({b})=\{a\in I\mid b(a)\not=0\}
is
finite. We use
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({I})
for the set of all finite multisets of elements of

I
. Given a finite family
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
of elements of
I
, we use
[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]
for the multiset
b
such that
b(a)=\#{\{i\mid a_{i}=a\}}
. We use
additive notations for multiset unions:
\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}
is the multiset

b
such that
b(a)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_{i}(a)
. The empty multiset is denoted as

0
or
[{\,}]
. If
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the multiset
kb
maps
a
to
k\,b(a)
.

Let
X
be a PCS. Given
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
and
b\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|})
, we define

u^{b}=\prod_{a\in|{X}|}u_{a}^{b(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Then we set
u^{!}=(u^{b})_{b\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|})}
and finally

\displaystyle\oc{X}=(\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|}),\{u^{!}\mid u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\}^{{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}})

which is a pre-PCS.

We check quickly that
\oc{X}
so defined is a PCS. Let
b=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|})
. Because
X
is a PCS, and by
Theorem
2
, for each
i=1,\dots,n
there is
u(i)\in\mathsf{P}{X}

such that
u(i)_{a_{i}}>0
. Let
(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be a family of strictly
positive real numbers such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}\leq 1
. Then

u=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}u(i)\in\mathsf{P}{X}
satisfies
u_{a_{i}}>0
for each

i=1,\dots,n
. Therefore
u^{!}_{b}=u^{b}>0
. This shows that there is

U\in\mathsf{P}{(\oc{X})}
such that
U_{b}>0
.

Let now
A\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
be
such that
\forall u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\,\forall i\in\{1,\dots,n\}\ u_{a_{i}}\leq A
. For
all
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
we have
u^{b}\leq A^{n}
. We have

\displaystyle(\mathsf{P}{(\oc{X})})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}={\{u^{!}\mid u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\}}^{{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}}=\{u^{!}\mid u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}\,.

Let
t\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{\oc{X}}|}
be defined by
t_{c}=0
if
c\not=b
and

t_{b}=A^{-n}>0
; we have
t\in(\mathsf{P}{(\oc{X})})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
. We have exhibited an
element
t
of
(\mathsf{P}{(\oc{X})})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
such that
t_{b}>0
. By
Lemma
1
it follows that
\oc{X}
is a PCS.

3.2.7.
Kleisli morphisms as functions.

Let
s\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{\oc{X}}\multimap{Y}}|}
. We define a function
\widehat{s}:\mathsf{P}{X}\to\overline{\mathbb{R}^{+}}^{|{Y}|}
as follows. Given
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, we set

\displaystyle\widehat{s}(u)=s\,{u^{!}}=\left(\sum_{c\in|{\oc{X}}|}s_{c,b}u^{c}\right)_{b\in|{Y}|}\,.

Theorem 6
.

One has
s\in\mathsf{P}{({\oc{X}}\multimap{Y})}
iff, for all
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, one has

\widehat{s}(u)\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
.

This is an immediate consequence of the definition.

Theorem 7
.

Let
s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X},Y)
. The function
\widehat{s}
is
Scott-continuous. Moreover, given
s,s^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X},Y)
, one has
s=s^{\prime}

(as matrices) iff
\widehat{s}=\widehat{s^{\prime}}
(as functions
\mathsf{P}{X}\to\mathsf{P}{Y}
).

This is an easy consequence of the fact that two polynomials of
n
variables
with real coefficients are identical iff they are the same function on any open
subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Terminology.
We say that
s\in\mathsf{P}{({\oc{X}}\multimap{Y})}
is a

power series
whose monomial
u^{c}
has coefficient

s_{c,b}
. Since
s
is characterized by the function
\widehat{s}
we
sometimes say that
\widehat{s}
is a power series.

We can consider the elements of
{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
(the morphisms of the
Kleisli category of the comonad
\oc{\_}
on the category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
) as
particular Scott continuous functions
\mathsf{P}{X}\to\mathsf{P}{Y}
and this
identification is compatible with the definition of identity maps and of
composition in
{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}
, see Section
3.1.2
. Of course, not all
Scott continuous function are morphisms in
{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
be such that
s\leq s^{\prime}
(as elements of

\mathsf{P}{({\oc{X}}\multimap{Y})}
). Then
\forall u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ \widehat{s}(u)\leq\widehat{s^{\prime}}(u)
. Let
(s(i))_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a monotone sequence of
elements of
{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
and let
s=\sup_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}s(i)
. Then
\forall u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ \widehat{s}(u)=\sup_{i\in I}\widehat{s_{i}}(u)
.

The first statement is obvious. The second one results from the monotone
convergence Theorem.

Given a multiset
b\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({I})
, we define its
factorial

{b}!=\prod_{i\in I}{b(i)}!
and its
multinomial
coefficient

\mathsf{mn}(b)={(\#{b})}!/{b}!\in{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
where

\#{b}=\sum_{i\in I}b(i)
is the cardinality of
b
. Remember that,
given an
I
-indexed family
a=(a_{i})_{i\in I}
of elements of a commutative
semi-ring, one has the multinomial formula

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}\Big)^{n}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{M}_{n}({I})}\mathsf{mn}(b)a^{b}

where
\mathcal{M}_{n}({I})=\{b\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({I})\mid\#{b}=n\}
.

Given
c\in|{\oc{X}}|
and
d\in|{\oc{Y}}|
we define

\mathsf{L}(c,d)
as the set of all multisets
r
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|\times|{Y}|})
such that

\displaystyle\forall a\in|{X}|\ \sum_{b\in|{Y}|}r(a,b)=c(a)\quad\text{and}\quad\forall b\in|{Y}|\ \sum_{a\in|{X}|}r(a,b)=d(b)\,.

Let
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
, we define
\oc{t}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{{\oc{X}}\multimap{\oc{Y}}}

by

\displaystyle(\oc{t})_{c,d}=\sum_{r\in\mathsf{L}(c,d)}\frac{{d}!}{{r}!}t^{r}\,.

Observe that the coefficients in this sum are all non-negative integers.

Lemma 9
.

For all
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
one has
\oc{t}\,u^{!}=(t\,u)^{!}
.

This results from a simple computation applying the multinomial formula.

Theorem 10
.

For all
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
one has
\oc{t}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X},\oc{Y})
and the
operation
t\mapsto\oc{t}
is functorial.

Immediate consequences of Lemma
9
and
Theorem
7
.

3.2.8.
Description of the exponential comonad.

We equip now this functor with a structure of comonad: let
\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{\oc{X}}\multimap{X}}|}
be given by
(\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X})_{b,a}=\delta_{{[{a}]},{b}}
(the value of the Kronecker symbol

\delta_{{i},{j}}
is
1
if
i=j
and
0
otherwise) and
\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{\oc{X}}\multimap{\oc{\oc{X}}}}|}
be given by
(\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X})_{b,[{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}}]}=\delta_{{\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}},{b}}
. Then we have

\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X},X)
and
\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X},\oc{\oc{X}})

simply because

\displaystyle\widehat{\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}}(u)=u\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X}}(u)=(u^{!})^{!}

for all
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, as easily checked. Using these equations, one also
checks easily the naturality of these morphisms, and the fact that

(\oc{\_},\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}},\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}})
is a comonad.

As to the monoidality of this comonad, we introduce

\mathrm{m}^{0}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{1}\multimap{\oc{\top}}}|}
by

\mathrm{m}^{0}_{*,[{}]}=1
and
\mathrm{m}^{2}_{X,Y}\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{{{\oc{X}}\otimes{\oc{Y}}}\multimap{\oc{({X}\mathrel{\&}{Y})}}}|}
by

(\mathrm{m}^{2}_{X,Y})_{b,c,d}=\delta_{{d},{1\cdot b+2\cdot c}}

where
i\cdot[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]=[{(i,a_{1}),\dots,(i,a_{n})}]
. It is easily
checked that the required commutations hold (again, we refer
to
[
24
]
).

3.2.9.
Fixpoints in
{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}
.

For any object
Y
of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
, a morphism
t\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(Y,Y)
defines a
Scott-continuous function
f=\widehat{t}:\mathsf{P}(Y)\to\mathsf{P}(Y)
which has a least
fixpoint
\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}f^{n}(0)
. Let
X
be an object of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
and set

Y={\oc({{\oc{X}}\multimap{X}})}\multimap{X}
. Then we have a morphism
t=\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}s\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(Y,Y)
where

s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\oc{Y}}\otimes{\oc({{\oc{X}}\multimap{X}})},X)
is defined as the following composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
:
{\oc{Y}}\otimes{\oc{({\oc{X}}\multimap{X})}}
\oc{Y}\otimes\oc{({\oc{X}}\multimap{X})}\otimes\oc{({\oc{X}}\multimap{X})}
\oc{X}\otimes({\oc{X}}\multimap{X})
X
s
{\oc{Y}}\otimes{\operatorname{\mathrm{c}}_{{\oc{X}}\multimap{X}}}
{(\operatorname{\mathrm{ev}}\,({\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{Y}}\otimes{\oc{({\oc{X}}\multimap{X})}}))^{!}}\otimes{\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{({\oc{X}}\multimap{X})}}
\operatorname{\mathrm{ev}}\,\sigma

Then
\widehat{t}
is a Scott continuous function

\mathsf{P}{Y}\to\mathsf{P}{Y}
whose least fixpoint is
{\mathrm{fix}}
, considered as a
morphism
{\mathrm{fix}}\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}({\oc{X}}\multimap{X},X)
, satisfies

\widehat{{\mathrm{fix}}}(u)=\sup_{n=0}^{\infty}\widehat{u}^{n}(0)
.

3.2.10.
The partially ordered class of probabilistic coherence spaces

We define the category
\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
. This category is actually a partially
ordered class whose objects are those of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
. The order relation, denoted
as
\subseteq
, is defined as follows:
X\subseteq Y
if
|{X}|\subseteq|{Y}|

and the matrices
\eta_{X,Y}^{+}
and
\eta_{X,Y}^{-}
defined, for

a\in|{X}|
and
b\in|{Y}|
, by
(\eta_{X,Y}^{+})_{a,b}=(\eta_{X,Y}^{-})_{b,a}=\delta_{{a},{b}}
satisfy
\eta_{X,Y}^{+}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
and

\eta_{X,Y}^{-}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Y,X)
. In other words: given
u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, the
element
{\eta_{X,Y}^{+}}\,{u}
of
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{Y}|}
obtained by
extending
u
with
0
’s outside
|{X}|
belongs to
\mathsf{P}{Y}
. And conversely,
given
v\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
, the element
{\eta_{X,Y}^{-}}\,{v}
of

(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X}|}
obtained by restricting
v
to
|{X}|
belongs to
\mathsf{P}{X}
. Considering
\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
as a category,
\eta_{X,Y}
is a
notation for the unique element of
\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}(X,Y)
when
X\subseteq Y
, in
accordance with the notations of Paragraph
3.1.3
.

Lemma 11
.

If
X\subseteq Y
then
X^{{\mathord{\perp}}}\subseteq Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
,
\eta_{X^{{\mathord{\perp}}},Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}^{+}=(\eta_{X,Y}^{-})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
and
\eta_{X^{{\mathord{\perp}}},Y^{{\mathord{\perp}}}}^{-}=(\eta_{X,Y}^{+})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
.

The proof is a straightforward verification.

We contend that
\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
is directed co-complete. Let

(X_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
be a countable directed family in
\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}

(so
\Gamma
is a countable directed poset and
\gamma\leq\gamma^{\prime}\Rightarrow X_{\gamma}\subseteq X_{\gamma^{\prime}}
), we have to check that this family has a least
upper bound
X
. We set
|{X}|=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}|{X_{\gamma}}|
and

\mathsf{P}{X}=\{w\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{X}|}\mid\forall\gamma\in\Gamma\ {\eta_{X,Y}^{-}}\,{w}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{\gamma}}\}
. This defines an object of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
which
satisfies
\mathsf{P}{X}=\{{\eta_{X_{\gamma},X}^{+}}\,{u}\mid\gamma\in\Gamma\text{ and
}u\in\mathsf{P}{X_{\gamma}}\}^{{\mathord{\perp}}{\mathord{\perp}}}
and is therefore the lub of the family

(X_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
in
\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
. This object
X
is denoted

\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}X_{\gamma}
. One checks easily that

(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}X_{\gamma})^{{\mathord{\perp}}}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}X_{\gamma}^{{\mathord{\perp}}}
.

Then the functor
\mathsf{E}:\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}\to\mathbf{Pcoh}
defined by
\mathsf{E}(X)=X
and

\mathsf{E}(\eta_{X,Y})=\eta_{X,Y}^{+}
is continuous: given a directed family

(X_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
whose lub is
X
and given a collection of
morphisms
t_{\gamma}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X_{\gamma},Y)
such that

t_{\gamma^{\prime}}\,\eta_{X_{\gamma},X_{\gamma^{\prime}}}^{+}=t_{\gamma}
for any

\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma
such that
\gamma\leq\gamma^{\prime}
, there is exactly one
morphism
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
such that

t\,\eta_{X_{\gamma},X}^{+}=t_{\gamma}

for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Given
a\in|{X}|
and
b\in|{Y}|
,

t_{a,b}=(t_{\gamma})_{a,b}
for any
\gamma
such that
a\in|{X_{\gamma}}|
(our
hypothesis on the
t_{\gamma}
’s means that
(t_{\gamma})_{a,b}
does not depend on
the choice of
\gamma
).

All the operations of Linear Logic define monotone continuous functionals on

\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
which moreover commute with the functor
\mathsf{F}
. This means
for instance that if
X\subseteq Y
then
\oc{X}\subseteq\oc{Y}
,

\eta_{\oc{X},\oc{Y}}^{+}=\oc{(\eta_{X,Y}^{+})}
,

\eta_{\oc{X},\oc{Y}}^{-}=\oc{(\eta_{X,Y}^{-})}
and

\oc{(\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}X_{\gamma})}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\oc{X_{\gamma}}
and similarly for
\otimes
and

\oplus
. As a consequence, and as a consequence of
Lemma
11
, if
X_{i}\subseteq Y_{i}
for

i=1,2
then
{X_{1}}\multimap{X_{2}}\subseteq{Y_{1}}\multimap{Y_{2}}
,

\eta_{{X_{1}}\multimap{X_{2}},{Y_{1}}\multimap{Y_{2}}}^{+}={\eta_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{-}}\multimap{\eta_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{+}}
and

\eta_{{X_{1}}\multimap{X_{2}},{Y_{1}}\multimap{Y_{2}}}^{-}={\eta_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{+}}\multimap{\eta_{X_{1},Y_{1}}^{-}}
and
\multimap

commutes with directed colimits in
\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
.

This notion of inclusion on probabilistic coherence spaces extends to
coalgebras as outlined in Section
3.1.3
(again, we refer
to
[
5
]
for more details). We describe briefly this notion of
inclusion in the present concrete setting.

Let
P
and
Q
be object of
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
, we have
P\subseteq Q
in

\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
if
\underline{P}\subseteq\underline{Q}
and
\mathrm{h}_{Q}\,\eta_{\underline{P},\underline{Q}}^{+}=\oc{(\eta_{\underline{P},\underline{Q}}^{+})}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}
. The lub of a directed family

(P_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
of coalgebras (for this notion of substructure)
is the coalgebra
P=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}P_{\gamma}
defined by
\underline{P}=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\underline{P_{\gamma}}
and
\mathrm{h}_{P}
is characterized
by the equation
\mathrm{h}_{P}\,\eta_{\underline{P_{\gamma}},\underline{P}}^{+}=\oc{\eta_{\underline{P_{\gamma}},\underline{P}}^{+}}\,\mathrm{h}_{P_{\gamma}}
which holds for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

As outlined in Section
3.1.4
, this allows to interpret
any type
\sigma
as an object
[{\sigma}]
of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
and any positive type

\varphi
as an object
[{\varphi}]^{\oc}
such that
\underline{[{\varphi}]^{\oc}}=[{\varphi}]
,
in such a way that the coalgebras
[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi}]^{\oc}
and

[{{\varphi}\left[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi}/{\varphi}\right]}]^{\oc}
are
exactly the same
objects of
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
. We use
\mathrm{h}_{\varphi}
for
\mathrm{h}_{[{\varphi}]^{\oc}}
.

3.2.11.
Dense coalgebras

Let
P
be an object of
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
, so that
P=(\underline{P},\mathrm{h}_{P})
where

\underline{P}
is a probabilistic coherence space and
\mathrm{h}_{P}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\underline{P},\oc{\underline{P}})
satisfies
\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{\underline{P}}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}=\oc{\mathrm{h}_{P}}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}
. Given coalgebras
P
and
Q
, a morphism

t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\underline{P},\underline{Q})
is coalgebraic (that is
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}(P,Q)
)
if
\mathrm{h}_{Q}\,t=\oc{t}\,\mathrm{h}_{P}
. In particular, we say that

u\in\mathsf{P}{(\underline{P})}
is coalgebraic if, considered as a morphism from

1
to
\underline{P}
,
u
belongs to
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}(1,P)
. This means that

u^{!}={\mathrm{h}_{P}}\,u
.

Definition 12
.

Given an object
P
of
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
, we use
\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P)}
for the set of
coalgebraic elements of
\mathsf{P}{(\underline{P})}
.

The following lemma is useful in the sequel and holds in any model of Linear
Logic.

Lemma 13
.

Let
X
be a probabilistic coherence space, one has
\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(\oc{X})}=\{u^{!}\mid u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\}
. Let
P_{\ell}
and
P_{r}
be objects of

\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
.

{P_{\ell}}\otimes{P_{r}}
is the cartesian product of
P_{\ell}
and
P_{r}

in
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
. The function

\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{\ell})}\times\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{r})}\to\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{({P_{\ell}}\otimes{P_{r}})}

which maps
(u,v)
to
{u}\otimes{v}
is a bijection. The projections

{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}^{\mathord{\otimes}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}({P_{\ell}}\otimes{P_{r}},P_{i})
are characterized by

{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}^{\mathord{\otimes}}({u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}})=u_{i}
.

The function

\{\ell\}\times\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{\ell})}\cup\{r\}\times\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{r})}\to\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{({P_{\ell}}\oplus{P_{r}})}

which maps
(i,u)
to
{\mathrm{in}}_{i}(u)
is a bijection. The injection

u\mapsto{\mathrm{in}}_{i}(u)
has a left inverse

{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\underline{P_{\ell}}}\oplus{\underline{P_{r}}},\underline{P_{i}})

defined by
({\mathrm{pr}}_{i})_{(j,a),b}=\delta_{{i},{j}}\delta_{{a},{b}}
,
which is not a coalgebra morphism in general.

Proof.

Let
v\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(\oc{X})}
, we have
v^{!}={\mathrm{h}_{\oc{X}}}\,v=\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X}\,v
hence
(\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}\,v)^{!}=\oc{\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}}\,v^{!}=\oc{\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{X}}\,\operatorname{{\mathrm{dig}}}_{X}v=v
. The
other properties result from the fact that the Eilenberg-Moore category

\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
is cartesian and co-cartesian with
\otimes
and
\oplus
as
product and co-product, see
[
24
]
for more details.
∎

Because of these properties we write sometimes
(u_{\ell},u_{r})
instead of

{u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}}
when
u_{i}\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{P_{i}}
for
i\in\{\ell,r\}
.

Definition 14
.

An object
P
of
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
is
dense
if, for any object

Y
of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
and any two morphisms
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\underline{P},Y)
, if
t\,u=t^{\prime}\,u
for all
u\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P)}
, then
t=t^{\prime}
.

Theorem 15
.

For any probabilistic coherence space
X
,
\oc{X}
is a dense
coalgebra. If
P_{\ell}
and
P_{r}
are dense coalgebras then
{P_{\ell}}\otimes{P_{r}}

and
{P_{\ell}}\oplus{P_{r}}
are dense. The colimit in

(\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}})_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
of a directed family of dense coalgebras is
dense.

Proof.

Let
X
be an object of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
, one has
\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(\oc{X})}=\{u^{!}\mid u\in\mathsf{P}{X}\}
by Lemma
13
. It follows that
\oc{X}

is a dense coalgebra by Theorem
7
. Assume that
P_{\ell}

and
P_{r}
are dense coalgebras. Let

t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\underline{P_{\ell}}}\otimes{\underline{P_{r}}},Y)
be such that
t\,w=t^{\prime}\,w
for all
w\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{({P_{\ell}}\otimes{P_{r}})}
. We have

\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}{(t)},\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}{(t^{\prime})}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\underline{P_{\ell}},{\underline{P}_{r}}\multimap{Y})
so,
using the density of
P_{\ell}
, it suffices to prove that
\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}{(t)}\,u_{\ell}=\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}{(t^{\prime})}\,u_{\ell}
for each
u_{\ell}\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{\ell})}
. So let

u_{\ell}\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{\ell})}
and let
s=\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}{(t)}\,u_{\ell}
and

s^{\prime}=\operatorname{\mathrm{cur}}{(t^{\prime})}\,u_{\ell}
. Let
u_{r}\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{r})}
, we have
s\,u_{r}=t\,({u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}})=t^{\prime}\,({u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}})=s^{\prime}\,u_{r}
since

{u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}}\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{({P_{\ell}}\otimes{P_{r}})}
and therefore
s=s^{\prime}
since

P_{r}
is dense. Let now
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\underline{P_{\ell}}}\oplus{\underline{P_{r}}},Y)

be such that
t\,w=t^{\prime}\,w
for all

w\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{({P_{\ell}}\oplus{P_{r}})}
. To prove that
t=t^{\prime}
, it suffices to prove
that
t\,{\mathrm{in}}_{i}=t^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
for
i\in\{\ell,r\}
. Since
P_{i}
is dense, it
suffices to prove that
t\,{\mathrm{in}}_{i}\,u=t^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{in}}_{i}\,u
for
each
u\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{i})}
which follows from the fact that
{\mathrm{in}}_{i}\,u\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{P_{i}}
. Last let
(P_{\gamma})_{\gamma\in\Gamma}
be a directed
family of dense coalgebras (in
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
) and let

P=\bigcup_{\gamma\in\Gamma}P_{\gamma}
, and let
t,t^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}(\underline{P},Y)

be such that
t\,w=t^{\prime}\,w
for all
w\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P)}
. It suffices
to prove that, for each
\gamma\in\Gamma
, one has

t\,\eta_{\underline{P_{\gamma}},\underline{P}}^{+}=t^{\prime}\,\eta_{\underline{P_{\gamma}},\underline{P}}^{+}
and this results
from the fact that
P_{\gamma}
is dense and

\eta_{\underline{P_{\gamma}},\underline{P}}^{+}
is a coalgebra morphisms (and
therefore maps
\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P_{\gamma})}
to
\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{(P)}
).
∎

The sub-category
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
of dense coalgebras is cartesian and
co-cartesian and is well-pointed by
Theorem
15
. We use
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathsf{den}}
for this
sub-category and
(\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathsf{den}})_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
for the sub-class of

\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
whose objects are the dense coalgebras (with the
same order relation).

3.2.12.
Interpreting types and terms in
\mathbf{Pcoh}

Given a type

with free type variables contained in the repetition-free

as an object of

\mathbf{Pcoh}
and when

is a positive type (whose free variables are contained
in

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle\zeta\hfil

) we define
[{\varphi}]^{\oc}_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern as an object of

\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
. These operations are continuous and their definition follows the
general pattern described in Section
3.1.4
.

Theorem 16
.

Let
\varphi
be a positive type and let
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}=(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{n})

be a repetition-free list of type variables which contains all the
free variables of
\varphi
. Let

Remark
.

It turns out that the interpretation of positive types in the
model
\mathbf{Pcoh}/\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
are dense coalgebras. This is mainly due to
the fact that the colimit of a directed family of dense coalgebras

in the partially ordered class
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
is dense,
see Theorem
15
. From the viewpoint of
Levy’s CBPV
[
22
]
, whose semantics is described in terms of
adjunctions, we are using a resolution of the comonad “
\oc
”
through the category
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}
(or, equivalently, through the
category
\mathbf{Pcoh}^{\mathord{\oc}}_{\mathsf{den}}
). As pointed out to us by one of the referees
and already mentioned in the introduction, there is another
resolution through a category of families and introduced
in
[
1
]
, which is initial among all resolutions that
model CBPV. This other option will be explored in further work.

Then
o={\mathbf{1}}\oplus{\mathbf{1}}
satisfies

|{[{o}]}|=\{(\ell,*),(r,*)\}
and

u\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{[{o}]}|}
satisfies
u\in\mathsf{P}{[{o}]}
iff

u_{(\ell,*)}+u_{(r,*)}\leq 1
. The coalgebraic structure of this
object is given by

\displaystyle(\mathrm{h}_{o})_{(j,*),[{(j_{1},*),\dots,(j_{k},*)}]}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }j=j_{1}=\dots=j_{k}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

The object
\mathsf{N}=[{\iota}]
satisfies

\mathsf{N}={1}\oplus{\mathsf{N}}
so that

|{\mathsf{N}}|=\{(\ell,*),(r,(\ell,*)),(r,(r,(\ell,*))),\dots\}

and we use
\overline{n}
for the element of
|{\mathsf{N}}|
which has
n

occurrences of
r
. Given
u\in(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{\mathsf{N}}|}
, we use
l(u)
for
the element of
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{|{\mathsf{N}}|}
defined by

l(u)_{\overline{n}}=u_{\overline{n+1}}
. By definition of
\mathsf{N}
, we have

u\in\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}}
iff
u_{\overline{0}}+\|{l(u)}\|_{\mathsf{N}}\leq 1
, and then

\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{N}}=u_{\overline{0}}+\|{l(u)}\|_{\mathsf{N}}
. It follows that

u\in\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}}
iff
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u_{\overline{n}}\leq 1
and of
course
\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{N}}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u_{\overline{n}}
. Then the
coalgebraic structure
\mathrm{h}_{\iota}
is defined exactly as

\mathrm{h}_{o}
above. In the sequel, we identify
|{\mathsf{N}}|
with

{\mathbb{N}}
.

Given a typing context
\mathcal{P}=(x_{1}:\varphi_{1},\dots,x_{k}:\varphi_{k})
, a type
\sigma
and a term

M
such that
\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\sigma
,
M
is interpreted as a morphism
\left[{M}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}([{\mathcal{P}}],[{\sigma}])
. For all constructs of the language but
probabilistic choice, this interpretation uses the generic structures of the
model described in Section
3.1
, the description of
this interpretation can be found in
[
5
]
.
We set
\left[{\mathsf{coin}(p)}\right]{}=pe_{(\ell,*)}+(1-p)e_{(r,*)}
.

If
x_{1}:\varphi_{1},\dots,x_{k}:\varphi_{k}\vdash M:\sigma
, the morphism
\left[{M}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}

is completely characterized by its values on

(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{([{\mathcal{P}}]^{\oc})}
. We describe now the interpretation of
terms using this observation.

•

\left[{()}\right]=1\in\mathsf{P}{1}=[0,1]
.

•

\left[{x_{i}}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=u_{i}
.

•

\left[{N^{\mathord{\oc}}}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=(\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k}))^{!}
.

•

\left[{\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right)}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})={\left[{M_{\ell}}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})}\otimes{\left[{M_{r}}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})}
.

•

\left[{\mathsf{in}_{i}N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})={\mathrm{in}}_{i}(\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k}))
,
i\in\{\ell,r\}
.

•

\left[{\mathsf{der}\,N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=\operatorname{{\mathrm{der}}}_{[{\sigma}]}(\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k}))
, assuming that
\mathcal{P}\vdash N:\oc\sigma
.

•

If
\mathcal{P}\vdash N:{\varphi}\multimap{\sigma}
and
\mathcal{P}\vdash R:\varphi
then

\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\in\mathsf{P}{({[{\varphi}]}\multimap{[{\sigma}]})}
,
and
\left[{R}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\in\mathsf{P}{([{\varphi}])}
and using the application of a matrix to a vector we have
\left[{\langle N\rangle R}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\,\left[{R}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})
.

•

If
\mathcal{P},x:\varphi\vdash N:\sigma
then
\left[{\lambda x^{\varphi}\,N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\in\mathsf{P}{({[{\varphi}]}\multimap{[{\sigma}]})}
is completely described
by the fact that, for all
u\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{([{\varphi}]^{\oc})}
, one has

\left[{\lambda x^{\varphi}\,N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})\,u=\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P},x:\varphi}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},u)
. This is a complete characterization of this interpretation by
Theorem
16
.

•

If
\mathcal{P}\vdash N:{\varphi_{\ell}}\oplus{\varphi_{r}}
and

\mathcal{P},y_{i}:\varphi_{i}\vdash R_{i}:\sigma
for
i\in\{\ell,r\}
, then

\left[{\mathsf{case}(N,y_{\ell}\cdot R_{\ell},y_{r}\cdot R_{r})}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=\left[{R_{\ell}}\right]^{\mathcal{P},y_{\ell}:\varphi_{\ell}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},{\mathrm{pr}}_{\ell}(\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})))+\left[{R_{r}}\right]^{\mathcal{P},y_{r}:\varphi_{r}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},{\mathrm{pr}}_{r}(\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})))
where
{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}{({P_{\ell}}\oplus{P_{r}},P_{i})}
is the
i
th
“projection” introduced in
3.2.11
, left inverse for
{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
.

•

If
\mathcal{P},x:\oc\sigma\vdash N:\sigma
then

\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P},x:\oc\sigma}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\underline{[{\mathcal{P}}]}}\otimes{\oc{[{\sigma}]}},[{\sigma}])
and

\left[{\mathsf{fix}\,x^{\oc\sigma}\,N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k})=\sup_{n=0}^{\infty}f^{n}(0)
where

f:\mathsf{P}{[{\sigma}]}\to\mathsf{P}{[{\sigma}]}
is the Scott-continuous function
given by
f(u)=\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P},x:\oc{\sigma}}(u_{1},\dots,u_{k},u^{!})
.

•

If
\mathcal{P}\vdash N:{\psi}\left[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi}/{\zeta}\right]
then
\left[{\mathsf{fold}(N)}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}=\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}
which makes sense since

[{{\psi}\left[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi}/{\zeta}\right]}]=[{\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi}]
.

•

If
\mathcal{P}\vdash N:\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi
then
\left[{\mathsf{unfold}(N)}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}=\left[{N}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}
.

Theorem 17

(Soundness)
.

If
M
satisfies
\mathcal{P}\vdash M:\sigma
then

\displaystyle\left[{M}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}=\sum_{\mathcal{P}\vdash M^{\prime}:\sigma}\mathsf{Red}_{M,M^{\prime}}\left[{M^{\prime}}\right]^{\mathcal{P}}

The proof is done by induction and is a straightforward verification.

Corollary 18
.

Let
M
be a term such that
{}\vdash{M}:{1}
so that
\left[{M}\right]\in[0,1]
. Then

\left[{M}\right]\geq\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{M,()}
.

This is an immediate consequence of Theorem
17
and of the
definition of
\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}
, see Section
2.2
.

\left[{\underline{n}}\right]=\overline{n}

4.
Adequacy

Our goal is to prove the converse of Corollary
18
: for any
closed term
M
such that
\vdash M:\mathbf{1}
, the probability that
M
reduces
to
()
is larger than or equal to

\left[{M}\right]{}\in\mathsf{P}{[{\mathbf{1}}]}\simeq[0,1]
, so that we shall know that these two
numbers are actually equal.

In spite of its very simple statement, the proof of this property is rather
long mainly because we have to deal with the recursive type definitions allowed
by our syntax. As usual, the proof is based on the definition of a logical
relations between terms and elements of the model (more precisely, given any
type
\sigma
, we have to define a relation between closed terms of types

\sigma
and elements of
\mathsf{P}{[{\sigma}]}
; let us call such a relation a

\sigma
-relation
).

Since we have no positivity restrictions on the occurrence of type variables
wrt. which recursive types are defined so that types are neither covariant nor
contravariant wrt. these type variables, we use a very powerful technique
introduced in
[
26
]
for defining this logical relation.

Indeed a type variable
\zeta
can have positive and negative
occurrences in a positive
13
13

13

Warning: the word “positive” has
two different meanings here!
type
\varphi
, consider for instance the
case
\varphi=\oc({\zeta}\multimap{\zeta})
where the type variable
\zeta

has a positive (on the right of the
{}\multimap{}
) and a negative
occurrence (on the left). To define the logical relation associated
with
\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi
, we have to find a fixpoint for the operation
which maps a
(\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi)
-relation
\mathcal{R}
to the relation

\Phi(\mathcal{R})=\oc({\mathcal{R}}\multimap{\mathcal{R}})
(which can be defined using

\mathcal{R}
as a “logical relation” in a fairly standard way). Relations
are naturally ordered by inclusion, and this strongly suggests to
define the above fixpoint using this order relation by
e.g.
Tarski’s
Fixpoint Theorem. The problem however is that
\Phi
is neither a
monotone nor an anti-monotone operation on relations, due to the fact
that
\zeta
has a positive and a negative occurrence in
\varphi
.

It is here that Pitts’s trick comes in: we replace the relations
\mathcal{R}
with
pairs of relations
\mathcal{R}=({\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}},{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}})
ordered as follows:

\mathcal{R}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{S}
if
{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathord{+}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathord{-}}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}}
.
Then we define accordingly
\Phi(\mathcal{R})
as a pair of relations by

{\Phi(\mathcal{R})}^{\mathord{-}}=\oc({{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}}}\multimap{{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}}})
and

{\Phi(\mathcal{R})}^{\mathord{+}}=\oc({{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}}}\multimap{{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}}})
. Now the operation

\Phi
is monotone wrt. the
\sqsubseteq
relation and it becomes possible to apply
Tarski’s Fixpoint Theorem to
\Phi
and get a pair of relations
\mathcal{R}
such that

\mathcal{R}=\Phi(\mathcal{R})
. The next step consists in proving that

{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}}={\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}}
. This is obtained by means of an analysis of the definition
of the interpretation of fixpoints of types as colimits in the category

\mathbf{Pcoh}_{\mathord{\subseteq}}
. One is finally in position of proving a fairly standard “Logical
Relation Lemma” from which adequacy follows straightforwardly.

In this short description of our adequacy proof, many technicalities have
obviously been hidden, the most important one being that values are handled in
a special way so that we actually consider two kinds of pairs of
relations. Also, a kind of “biorthogonality closure” plays an essential role
in the handling of positive types, no surprise for the readers acquainted with
Linear Logic, see for instance the proof of normalization in
[
12
]
.

4.1.
Pairs of relations and basic operations

Given a
closed
type
\sigma
, we define
\mathsf{Rel}(\sigma)
as the set of all
pairs of relations
\mathcal{R}=({\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}},{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}})
such that, for

\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{+},\mathord{-}\}
, each element of
\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}
is a pair

(M,u)
where
\vdash M:\sigma
and
u\in\mathsf{P}{[{\sigma}]}
. For a

closed
positive type
\varphi
, we also define
\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi)
as the set of
all pairs of relations
\mathcal{V}=({\mathcal{V}}^{\mathord{-}},{\mathcal{V}}^{\mathord{+}})
such that, for

\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{+},\mathord{-}\}
, each element of
\mathcal{V}^{\varepsilon}
is a pair

(V,v)
where
\vdash V:\varphi
is a value and
v\in\mathsf{P}^{\mathord{\oc}}{[{\varphi}]}
.

Given
\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\mathsf{Rel}(\sigma)
, we write
\mathcal{R}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{S}
if

{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}}\subseteq{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathord{+}}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\mathord{-}}\subseteq{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}}
. We define similarly

\mathcal{V}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{W}
for
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{W}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi)
. Then
\mathsf{Rel}(\sigma)
is a complete
meet-lattice, the infimum of a collection
(\mathcal{R}_{i})_{i\in I}
being

\bigsqcap_{i\in I}\mathcal{R}_{i}=(\bigcup_{i\in I}{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{-}}_{i},\bigcap_{i\in I}{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathord{+}}_{i})
. The same holds of course for
\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi)
and we use the same
notations.

We define
\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
as the set of all pairs
(M,p)
such that

\vdash M:\mathbf{1}
,
p\in[0,1]
and
\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{M,()}\geq p
.

We define in Figure
4
logical operations on these
pairs of relations. The last one is the aforementioned
biorthogonality closure operation on pairs of relations.

•

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\mathsf{Rel}(\sigma)
, we define
\oc\mathcal{R}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\oc\sigma)
by:

\oc\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}=\{(M^{\mathord{\oc}},u^{!})\mid(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}\}

for
\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{-},\mathord{+}\}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{V}_{i}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi_{i})
for
i\in\{\ell,r\}
. We define

({\mathcal{V}_{\ell}}\otimes{\mathcal{V}_{r}})^{\varepsilon}=\{(\left(V_{\ell},V_{r}\right),{v_{\ell}}\otimes{v_{r}})\}\mid(V_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\varepsilon}\}
for
\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{-},\mathord{+}\}
, so that

{\mathcal{V}_{\ell}}\otimes{\mathcal{V}_{r}}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}({\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}})
.

•

Let
\mathcal{V}_{i}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi_{i})
for
i\in\{\ell,r\}
. We define

({\mathcal{V}_{\ell}}\oplus{\mathcal{V}_{r}})^{\varepsilon}=\{(\mathsf{in}_{i}V,{\mathrm{in}}_{i}(v))\}\mid i\in\{\ell,r\}\text{ and }(V,v)\in\mathcal{V}_{i}^{\varepsilon}\}
for

\varepsilon\in\{\mathord{-},\mathord{+}\}
, so that

{\mathcal{V}_{\ell}}\oplus{\mathcal{V}_{r}}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}({\varphi_{\ell}}\oplus{\varphi_{r}})
.

•

Let
\mathcal{V}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi)
and
\mathcal{R}\in\mathsf{Rel}(\sigma)
. We define

{\mathcal{V}}\multimap{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathsf{Rel}({\varphi}\multimap{\sigma})
as follows:

({\mathcal{V}}\multimap{\mathcal{R}})^{\varepsilon}=\{(M,u)\mid\ \vdash M:{\varphi}\multimap{\sigma},\ u\in\mathsf{P}{[{{\varphi}\multimap{\sigma}}]}\text{ and }\forall(V,v)\in\mathcal{V}^{-\varepsilon}\ (\langle M\rangle V,u{v}\,{)}\in\mathcal{R}^{\varepsilon}\}
.

•

Last, given
\mathcal{V}\in\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi)
, we define
\overline{\mathcal{V}}\in\mathsf{Rel}(\varphi)
as
follows:
\overline{\mathcal{V}}^{\varepsilon}
is the set of all
(M,u)
such that

\vdash M:\varphi
,
u\in\mathsf{P}{[{\varphi}]}
and, for all

(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}}\multimap{\mathcal{R}}(\mathbf{1}))^{-\varepsilon}
, one has
(\langle T\rangle M,{t}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
.

Figure 4.
Logical operations for pairs of relations

Observe that all these operations are monotone wrt.
\sqsubseteq
. For instance

\mathcal{V}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{W}\mathrel{\wedge}\mathcal{R}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{S}\Rightarrow({\mathcal{V}}\multimap{\mathcal{R}})\sqsubseteq({\mathcal{W}}\multimap{\mathcal{S}})
,
and
\mathcal{V}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{W}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{V}}\sqsubseteq\overline{\mathcal{W}}
.

4.2.
Fixpoints of pairs of relations

To deal with fixpoint types
\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\varphi
, we need to consider types
parameterized by relations as follows.

The definition is by simultaneous induction on
\sigma
and
\varphi
. All cases
but one consist in applying straightforwardly the above defined logical
operations on pairs of relations, for instance

We recall the statement of Tarski’s fixpoint theorem.

Theorem 19
.

Let
S
and
T
be complete meet semi-lattices and let
f:S\times T\to T
be
a monotone function. For
x\in S
, let
g(x)
be the meet of the set
\{y\in T\mid f(x,y)\leq y\}
. Then the function
g
is monotone and satisfies

f(x,g(x))=g(x)
for each
x\in S
.

Applying this theorem we obtain, by induction on types, the following property.

Lemma 20
.

For any type
\sigma
and any positive type
\varphi
, the maps

satisfies

\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{fold}(\mathcal{R}(\psi)_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

4.3.
Some useful closeness lemmas

We state and prove a series of lemmas expressing that our pairs of relations
are closed under various syntactic and semantic operations.

Lemma 21
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be terms such that
\vdash M:\mathbf{1}
and

\vdash M^{\prime}:\mathbf{1}
. If
M\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}M^{\prime}
then
\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{M,()}=\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{M^{\prime},()}
.

This is straightforward since any reduction path from
M
to
()
must
start with the step
M\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}M^{\prime}
, and this is a probability
1
step.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\varphi
be a closed positive type and let
\sigma
be a closed type. Let

(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}(\varphi)^{-\varepsilon}
and

(R,r)\in\mathcal{R}({\varphi}\multimap{\sigma})^{\varepsilon}
. Then
(\langle R\rangle M,{r}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

We can write
\sigma={\varphi_{1}}\multimap{{\cdots}\multimap{{\varphi_{n}}\multimap{\psi}}}
for
some
n
and
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n},\psi
positive and closed. Given

(V_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{i})^{-\varepsilon}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, we have
to prove that

\displaystyle(\langle R\rangle M\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n},{r}\,{u}\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n})\in(\overline{\mathcal{V}(\psi)})^{\varepsilon}

so let
(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}(\psi)}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
, we
have to prove that

\displaystyle(\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle M\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n}),t({r}\,{u}\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n}))\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})\,.

Let
S=\lambda x^{\varphi}\,\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle x\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})
so that

\vdash S:{\varphi}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}
. Similarly let

s\in\mathsf{P}{[{{\varphi}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}}]}
be the linear morphism defined by

{s}\,{u^{\prime}}=t({r}\,{u^{\prime}}\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n})
(the fact that
s
so
defined is actually a morphism in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
results from the symmetric
monoidal closeness of that category and from the fact that
r
and

t
are morphisms in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
).
Let
(V,v)\in\mathcal{V}(\varphi)^{-\varepsilon}
, we have

(\langle R\rangle V,{r}\,{v})\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\varepsilon}
and hence

(\langle R\rangle V\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n}),{r}\,{v}\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n}\in\mathcal{R}(\psi)^{\varepsilon}
. Therefore

\displaystyle(\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle V\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})),t({r}\,{v}\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})

since we have assumed that

(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}(\psi)}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
. Since

t({r}\,{v}\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n})={s}\,{v}
, and by
Lemma
21
, it follows that

(\langle S\rangle V,{s}\,{v})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
. Hence

(S,s)\in\mathcal{R}({\varphi}\multimap{\mathbf{1}})^{\varepsilon}
and therefore

(\langle S\rangle M,{s}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
since we have

(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}(\varphi)^{-\varepsilon}
.

We finish the proof by observing that

{s}\,{u}=t({r}\,{u}\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n})
and by showing that

\displaystyle\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{\langle T\rangle\langle R\rangle M\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n},()}=\mathsf{Red}^{\infty}_{\langle S\rangle M,()}

For this it suffices to observe (by inspection of the reduction rules) that
each reduction path
\pi
from
\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle M\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})
to

()
is of shape
\pi=\lambda\rho
where

•

\lambda
is a reduction path

\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle M_{1}\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle M_{2}\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle M_{k+1}\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})

where
M_{1}=M
,
M_{k+1}
is a value
V
and

M=M_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}}M_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}}M_{k+1}=V

•

and
\rho
is a reduction path from
\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle V\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})
to
()
.

Then we have
\langle S\rangle M=\langle S\rangle M_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle S\rangle M_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle S\rangle V\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle V\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})
, the last step resulting from the
definition of
S
. In that way, we have defined a probability preserving
bijection between the reduction paths from
\langle T\rangle(\langle R\rangle M_{1}\,V_{1}\cdots V_{n})
to
()
and the reduction paths from
\langle S\rangle M
to

()
, proving our contention.
∎

Lemma 23
.

Let
\varphi_{i}
be closed positive types and

(M_{i},u_{i})\in\mathcal{R}(\varphi_{i})^{\varepsilon}
for
i\in\{\ell,r\}
. Then

(\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right),{u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}})\in\mathcal{R}({\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}})^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Let

(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}({\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}})}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
,
we must prove that

(\langle T\rangle\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right),t({u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}}))\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
.
Let

S=\lambda x_{\ell}^{\varphi_{\ell}}\,\lambda x_{r}^{\varphi_{r}}\,\langle T\rangle\left(x_{\ell},x_{r}\right)

and
s\in\mathsf{P}{[{{\varphi_{\ell}}\multimap{({\varphi_{r}}\multimap{\mathbf{1}})}}]}
be
defined by
{s}\,{u_{\ell}}\,u_{r}=t({u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}})

(again,
s
is a morphism in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
by symmetric monoidal closeness
of that category). It is clear that

(S,s)\in({\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{\ell})}\multimap{({\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{r})}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})}{})}{})^{-\varepsilon}
.
By Lemma
22
we get

(\langle S\rangle M_{\ell},{s}\,{u_{\ell}})\in({\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{r})}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}

and then

(\langle S\rangle M_{\ell}\,M_{r},t({u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
. Observing
that there is a probability preserving bijection between the
reduction paths from
\langle S\rangle M_{\ell}\,M_{r}
to
()
and the
reduction paths from
\langle T\rangle\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right)
to
()
, we
conclude that

(\langle T\rangle\left(M_{\ell},M_{r}\right),t({u_{\ell}}\otimes{u_{r}}))\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})

as contended (in both terms one has to reduce first
M_{\ell}
and
then
M_{r}
to a value).
∎

Lemma 24
.

Let
\varphi_{\ell}
and
\varphi_{r}
be closed positive types. If

(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}({\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}})^{\varepsilon}
then

(\mathsf{pr}_{i}M,{{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\varphi_{i})^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Let

(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{i})}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
,
we have to prove that

(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}M,{t}\,{({{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}}\,{u})})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
.
Let
S=\lambda x^{{\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}}}\,\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}x
and

s\in\mathsf{P}{[{{{\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}}}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}}]}
be defined
by
{s}\,{u_{0}}={t}\,{({{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}}\,{u_{0}})}
for all

u_{0}\in\mathsf{P}{[{{\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}}}]}
. Let

(W,w)\in\mathcal{V}({\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}})^{\varepsilon}
, which
means that
W=\left(V_{\ell},V_{r}\right)
and
w={v_{\ell}}\otimes{v_{r}}
with

(V_{j},v_{j})\in\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{j})^{\varepsilon}
for

j\in\{\ell,r\}
. We have
\langle S\rangle W\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}\langle T\rangle V_{i}
and

{s}\,{w}={t}\,{v_{i}}
and we know that

(\langle T\rangle V_{i},{t}\,{v_{i}})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
from which it follows
by Lemma
21
that

(\langle S\rangle W,{s}\,{w})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
. So we have proven that

(S,s)\in({\mathcal{V}({\varphi_{\ell}}\otimes{\varphi_{r}})}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}

and hence
(\langle S\rangle M,{s}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
. We have

{s}\,{u}={t}\,{({{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}}\,{u})}
. Moreover we have a
probability preserving bijection between the reduction paths from

\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}M
to
()
and the reduction paths from

\langle S\rangle M
to
()
, and hence we have

(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}M,{t}\,{({{\mathrm{pr}}_{i}}\,{u})})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
as
contended.

Indeed, any reduction path
\pi
from
\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}M
to
()
has
shape
\pi=\lambda\rho
where
\lambda
is a reduction path
\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}M=\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}M_{\ell}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}M_{r}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}W\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle V_{i}
(with
W=\left(V_{\ell},V_{r}\right)
) and
\rho
is
a reduction path from
\langle T\rangle V_{i}
to
()
. The first steps
\lambda

of this reduction are determined by the reduction path

M=M_{\ell}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}}W
from
M
to the value

W
. This reduction path determines uniquely the reduction path
\langle S\rangle M=\langle S\rangle M_{\ell}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle S\rangle W\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle\mathsf{pr}_{i}W\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle 1}}{{\rightarrow}}}\langle T\rangle V_{i}
followed by the reduction

\rho
from
\langle T\rangle V_{i}
to
()
by
\rho
.
∎

Lemma 25
.

Let
\varphi_{\ell}
and
\varphi_{r}
be closed positive types and let

(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}(\varphi_{i})^{\varepsilon}
for
i=\ell
or
i=r
. Then
(\mathsf{in}_{i}M,{{\mathrm{in}}_{i}}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}({\varphi_{\ell}}\oplus{\varphi_{r}})^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Let

(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}({\varphi_{\ell}}\oplus{\varphi_{r}})}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
,
we must prove that

(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{in}_{i}M,{t}\,{({{\mathrm{in}}_{i}}\,{u})})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
.
Let
S=\lambda x^{\varphi_{i}}\,\langle t\rangle\mathsf{in}_{i}(x)
and let

s\in\mathsf{P}{[{{\varphi_{i}}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}}]}
. It is clear that

(S,s)\in({\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{i})}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})}{})^{-\varepsilon}

and it follows that
(\langle S\rangle M,{s}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
which
implies

(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{in}_{i}M,{t}\,{({{\mathrm{in}}_{i}}\,{u})})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})

by the usual bijective and probability preserving bijection on
reductions.
∎

The next lemma uses notations introduced in Section
3.2.5
.

Lemma 26
.

Let
\varphi_{\ell}
and
\varphi_{r}
be closed positive type and
\sigma
be a closed
type. Let
(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}({\varphi_{\ell}}\oplus{\varphi_{r}})^{\varepsilon}
. For

i\in\{\ell,r\}
, let
R_{i}
be a term such that

y_{i}:\varphi_{i}\vdash R_{i}:\sigma
and assume that

(\lambda x_{i}^{\varphi_{i}}\,R_{i},r_{i})\in\mathcal{R}({\varphi_{i}}\multimap{\sigma})^{-\varepsilon}
. Then

(\mathsf{case}(M,y_{\ell}\cdot R_{\ell},y_{r}\cdot R_{r}),{\mathrm{case}(r_{\ell},r_{r})}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{-\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

We can write
\sigma={\psi_{1}}\multimap{{\cdots}\multimap{{\psi_{k}}\multimap{\psi}}}

where
\psi
and the
\psi_{j}
’s are closed and positive types. Given

(W_{j},w_{j})\in\mathcal{V}(\psi_{j})^{\varepsilon}
for
j=1,\dots,k
, we have to
prove that

\displaystyle(\langle\mathsf{case}(M,y_{\ell}\cdot R_{\ell},y_{r}\cdot R_{r})\rangle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}},{\mathrm{case}(r_{\ell},r_{r})}\,{u}\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern it follows that

(\langle S\rangle M,{s}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
. There is a bijective and
probability preserving correspondence between the reductions from

and the reductions from

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}

to
()
: as usual such reductions start with a reduction

M=M_{1}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}}M_{2}\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}}\cdots\mathrel{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p_{k}}}{{\rightarrow}}}M_{k}=\mathsf{in}_{i}V

where
i\in\{\ell,r\}
and
V
is a value of type
\varphi_{i}
and
(after a few
\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}
-steps) continue with a reduction from

\langle T\rangle\langle R_{i}\rangle V\,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle W\hfil$\crcr}}}
to

()
. Therefore (
4.3
) holds and hence we
have (
4.3
), this ends the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 27
.

\sigma
be a closed type and let

Lemma 28
.

Let
\varphi
be a closed positive type of shape
\varphi=\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi
. If

(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}(\varphi)^{\varepsilon}
then
(\mathsf{unfold}(M),u)\in\mathcal{R}({\psi}\left[{\varphi}/{\zeta}\right])^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Let

(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}({\psi}\left[{\varphi}/{\zeta}\right])}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
,
we must prove that
(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{unfold}(M),u)\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
. As
usual one defines
S=\lambda x^{\varphi}\,\langle T\rangle\mathsf{unfold}(x)
and one
proves that

(S,t)\in({\mathcal{V}(\varphi)}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
.
This results from the fact that if

(V,v)\in\mathcal{V}(\varphi)^{\varepsilon}
then
V=\mathsf{fold}(W)
with

(W,v)\in\mathcal{V}({\psi}\left[{\varphi}/{\zeta}\right])^{\varepsilon}
, from
Lemma
21
and from the fact that

\mathsf{unfold}(\mathsf{fold}(W))\mathrel{\mathord{\to}_{\mathsf{w}}}W
(and of course from our assumption on

(T,t)
). It follows that
(\langle S\rangle M,{t}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})

from which we deduce
(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{unfold}(M),u)\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
by the
usual reasoning involving a bijective probability preserving
correspondence on reductions.
∎

Lemma 29
.

Let
\varphi
be a closed positive type of shape
\varphi=\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi
. If

(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}({\psi}\left[{\varphi}/{\zeta}\right])^{\varepsilon}
then
(\mathsf{fold}(M),u)\in\mathcal{R}(\varphi)^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

Let

(T,t)\in({\mathcal{V}(\varphi)}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
,
we must prove that
(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{fold}(M),u)\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
. As usual
one defines
S=\lambda x^{{\psi}\left[{\varphi}/{\zeta}\right]}\,\langle T\rangle\mathsf{fold}(x)
and
one proves that

(S,t)\in({\mathcal{V}({\psi}\left[{\varphi}/{\zeta}\right])}\multimap{\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})})^{-\varepsilon}
.
This results easily from the fact that, if

(V,v)\in\mathcal{V}({\psi}\left[{\varphi}/{\zeta}\right])^{\varepsilon}
then

(\mathsf{fold}(V),v)\in\mathcal{V}(\varphi)^{\varepsilon}
, from
Lemma
21
and from our assumption about

(T,t)
. Therefore we have
(\langle S\rangle M,{t}\,{u})\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1}))

from which we deduce
(\langle T\rangle\mathsf{fold}(M),u)\in\mathcal{R}(\mathbf{1})
by the
usual reasoning.
∎

Lemma 30
.

Let
\sigma
be a closed type and let
M
be a closed term of type

\sigma
. Then
(M,0)\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\varepsilon}
and, if

D\subseteq\mathsf{P}{[{\sigma}]}
is directed and satisfies
\forall u\in D\ (M,u)\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\varepsilon}
then
(M,\sup D)\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\varepsilon}
. Last, if

(M,u)\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\varepsilon}
and
u^{\prime}\leq u
then

(M,u^{\prime})\in\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\varepsilon}
.

Proof.

We can write

\sigma={\varphi_{1}}\multimap{{\cdots}\multimap{{\varphi_{n}}\multimap{\psi}}}
for some
n
and

\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n},\psi
positive and closed. Let us prove the second
statement. For
i=1,\dots,n
, let

(V_{i},v_{i})\in\mathcal{V}(\varphi_{i})^{-\varepsilon}
, we must prove that
(\langle M\rangle V_{1}\cdots V_{n},{(\sup D)}\,{v_{1}}\cdots v_{n})\in\overline{\mathcal{V}(\psi)}^{\varepsilon}
, knowing that

\forall u\in D\quad(\langle M\rangle V_{1}\cdots V_{n},u\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n})\in\overline{\mathcal{V}(\psi)}^{\varepsilon}\,.

This results from the fact that, given

t\in\mathsf{P}{[{{\psi}\multimap{\mathbf{1}}}]}
, the map

u\mapsto{t}\,{(u\,v_{1}\cdots v_{n})}

is Scott continuous from
\mathsf{P}{[{\varphi}]}
to
[0,1]
. The first statement
of the lemma results from the fact that this function maps
0
to
0
. The
last one results from the fact that this function is monotone.
∎

4.4.
Uniqueness of the relation

With any closed type
\sigma
we have associated a pair of relations

\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. We need now to prove that this pair satisfies

\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\mathord{+}}=\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\mathord{-}}
so that we have actually
associated a unique relation with any type.

To this end we prove first that

\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\mathord{+}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}(\sigma)^{\mathord{-}}
. Defining, for any
pair of relations
\mathcal{R}
, the relation
{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{op}}
as
(\mathcal{R}^{\mathord{+}},\mathcal{R}^{\mathord{-}})
,
this amounts to proving that
{\mathcal{R}(\sigma)}\sqsubseteq{\mathcal{R}(\sigma)}^{\mathsf{op}}
. We use
the same notation for the elements of
\mathsf{Rel}^{\mathsf{v}}(\varphi)
for
\varphi
positive.

For the next lemma, we use the same notational conventions as above.

Lemma 31

Proof.

The proof is by induction on types. All cases result straightforwardly from
the monotony of the logical operations on pairs of relations, but the case of
fixpoints of types. So assume that
\varphi=\textsf{Rec}\,\zeta\cdot\psi
, let

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathcal{V}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern since
\mathcal{V}=\mathsf{fold}(\mathcal{V}(\psi)_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\zeta\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern
